#170. 'An annoying buzz': The search for stealthier drones

Amazon Prime Air is expanding to 500 US towns and cities

Nature lover Ashley Schmidt no longer spends much time in her garden, in the Dallas suburb of North Garland.

She's also given up on cycling to the local parks where she previously enjoyed taking close-up photos of the bugs, lizards and toads.

It's all because she can't stand the sound of the delivery drones that fly overhead, depositing her neighbours' online orders.

"They emit this very annoying buzz. It triggers my fight or flight response," says the 44-year-old administrative assistant.

"You can hear the drones on approach and you can hear them inside your home when they pass directly overhead and then you hear them as they leave," she says.

Amazon says it is working to reduce the noise of its drone fleet

Schmidt is one of a growing number of people living with the noise of drones dispatched by companies including Amazon and Walmart as they seek to speed up home deliveries.

Delivery drone services, not long ago a novelty, are becoming part of everyday life for many people in the US.

Among them, Amazon Prime Air is in the process of expanding deliveries to nearly 500 cities and towns, delivering items that fit into a space the size of a shoe box. It has also launched to customers in and around Darlington in the north of England.

Smaller drone delivery services are popping up too: in some US cities, customers can order flying treats from their local pizza joint or ice cream parlour.

Even in cities where noise is already a steady part of life, the sound of drones seems to irk in a particular way.

While the steady passing of traffic might blend into the background, the sudden whine of a drone's rotors can pierce the everyday hum.

The sound tends to be "particularly noticeable, even when overall sound levels are relatively low," says Antonio Torija Martinez, professor in acoustic engineering and psychoacoustics at the University of Salford.

Unlike planes, drones operate at low altitudes and may fly directly over residential areas.

"They can expose communities that have not previously experienced significant aircraft noise, and who may therefore be more sensitive to changes in their local soundscape," says Torija Martinez.

He points to research showing that people respond strongly to a noise when they consider the source to be "novel, intrusive or frequent". People who benefit from a drone delivery service, for example, may be less bothered by the noise.

However, complaints about the sound of drones are likely to grow, he warns: "At the moment we are seeing some of these problems localised in some areas, butâ¦ as the drone sector expands, more and more of these complaints will happen."

In the US, the Federal Aviation Administration (FAA) regulates commercial drones, but it has come under fire for allowing noisy drone delivery operations. The FAA says operators and local community leaders have primary responsibility for addressing any community concerns.

Greenjets encloses some of the blades on its drone inside a pod

Might better technology muffle the irritating buzz?

One startup, Greenjets, set out to tackle both "noise level and the frequency of that noise," as Darren James, future technologies lead at the company, puts it.

The London-based business has designed a propulsion system it claims can reduce drone noise by at least two thirds.

While standard drones use spinning propellers that slice through open air, creating that high-pitched buzz, Greenjets encloses spinning blades inside a pod, to muffle the sound and direct the noise away from the ground.

Its system also quietens things down through slower rotor speed and by using materials that reduce vibration noise.

Making quieter drones isn't just about keeping the noise down for neighbours who are already sick of airborne deliveries.

Greenjets' low-noise tech has been used by police and harbour security teams in Germany. It has also helped conservationists in Africa, who are seeking to track poachers from above without being detected.

Battlefield drone operators also have good reason to keep the noise down, to avoid alerting the enemy of the approach of their unmanned vehicles.

"There are some efforts to reduce the sound of drones. The US Special Forces are interested, at an experimental stage," says Robert Tollast, a research fellow at Whitehall think tank The Royal United Services Institute (RUSI).

But he stresses that drone speed, rather than stealthiness, tends to come first for larger drones used in the war between Russia and Ukraine. "Make it faster, not less quiet, seems to be the approach," says Tollast.

Sharrow Engineering developed twisted propellers that reduce noise

For Detroit-based entrepreneur, Greg Sharrow, it was the challenge of broadcasting a classical music performance back in 2012 that set him on the path to quieter drone design.

He needed to film a violinist from a tricky angle and a drone seemed the only solution â except that it would be far too noisy.

As a starting point, "I knew that most of the noise from the drone was coming from the propeller, not the motor," he says. Specifically, it was the tips of the blades that he realised were creating the noise as they swiped through the air.

"I asked myself a very simple question, what would a tipless propeller look like and how would it function?"

The result was to create a propeller with twisted loop-shaped blades with no points.

It turned out it was easier to test his propeller in water than it was to repeatedly fly a drone, which led Sharrow to initially develop a less noisy boat propeller. Now, however, his company, Sharrow Engineering, is focusing on developing "ultra-quiet" drone rotors.

"We have validated that the technology works in drone applications and that it is dramatically quieter," says Sharrow.

Companies at the forefront of drone deliveries say they are also working to reduce the noise of their fleets. A spokesperson for Amazon says it has made "measurable sound improvements" to its drones and its latest designs use custom-designed propellers "that produce almost half the perceived sound of the prior generation, with a milder tone".

Drone maker Wing, which provides its vehicles for Walmart deliveries, says its propellers are designed to minimise noise. It also varies its flight paths to prevent repeated overflights over any single home or neighbourhood.

For Schmidt, fed up with the noise of drones, the only truly satisfactory solution would be to halt drone deliveries in her area. "I would like them to completely stop until they figure out how their services stop being a nuisance," she says.

X-planes: Are they needed in the new era of drones?

#168. 'I live with a ghost' - inside the haunted homes attracting bargain hunters

The previous occupant of Ryua's apartment, pictured here, sadly took their own life

From the moment Ryua first walked into her rental apartment in Tokyo she believed it to be haunted.

"When I visited the place for a viewing there was a man standing there, glaring at me," she says.

"Since the estate agent couldn't see him, I realised he must be a ghost."

While someone more squeamish would probably have quickly left the flat never to return, Ryua chose to go ahead and move in.

"I'm a bit of a contrarian, so I decided I wanted to become friends with this glaring male ghost," adds Ryua, who works as an aidoru, a young entertainer, and who doesn't wish to share her surname.

Four years later, and still in the same apartment, she says she continues to experience paranormal activity on a daily basis. She says she hears endless footsteps and the ghost talking.

Ryua says that she shares her Tokyo apartment with a ghost

Although Japan has increasingly marked Halloween, external in recent years, many Japanese people take ghosts seriously all year round. The country has a word, "yuurei", which means a lingering, troubled spirit.

These are the ghosts of people who died in violent or tragic circumstances - those who were murdered, killed themselves, or simply were lonely and died alone. If they died at home, then the belief is that their yuurei won't go away.

For house hunters who are not superstitious or squeamish it means there are big bargains to be found. There is a whole category of dwellings called jiko bukken, which means "stigmatised" or "incident" properties.

Under guidelines issued by the government in 2021, Japanese estate agents must disclose suicide, murder, death by fire, and any death where the body was not discovered for an extended period of time.

For rental properties this information must be disclosed for three years, and it must be revealed indefinitely for any property offered for sale.

By contrast, in the UK there is no legal requirement, external to reveal that a person died in a property. And in the US, it differs from state to state., external For example, in California you have to reveal if a person has died in the property in the past three years, while in Arizona and Oregon you do not.

Ryua was told that a man had killed himself in her apartment. It meant that she gets an almost 30% discount on her rent on her tiny, 25 sq m bedsit in the fashionable ward of Setagaya. She says she pays 45,000 yen ($285; Â£213) a month, instead of what should have been 62,000 yen.

While a house where there had been a murder or suicide would probably be harder to sell or rent out anywhere in the world, Tokyo estate agent Erik Oskamp says that Japanese people are particularly superstitious and averse to stigmatised properties.

"[Even] an apartment next to a cemetery will be difficult to rent out, especially if you can see the tombs from the kitchen," explains Oskamp. "The worst thing is if a suicide has happened in the apartment. Even if somebody has jumped from the roof, many renters will move out. Other issues are a serious crime or a lonely death."

The latter issue, that of a person dying alone, would not generally affect the price of a property in, say, the UK or the US.

But in rural Kagoshima, in Japan's southwest, it enabled Diane Tincher to buy a large three-bedroom house for about half of what would have otherwise been the asking price.

The 69-year-old American has lived in Japan for nearly 40 years, working as a tour guide. She says that the estate agent had been straight with her â there was a heavy discount as an elderly lady had died alone, thereby stigmatising the property.

Tincher paid 20m yen for the house, in a location where similar properties usually sell for up to 45m yen.

"I don't believe in ghosts. But I know that the Japanese are very superstitious," she says. "If there is a ghost I'm thankful to her every day... to live in such a splendid and affordable home."

Diane Tincher says she got a 50% reduction on her home in rural Japan

Researching the house, Tincher had found it on a website called Oshimaland, which lists jiko bukken properties, giving details of the events that occurred. It was launched in 2005 covering Tokyo, before expanding across Japan and then internationally in 2011.

It is crowdsourced, meaning that members of the public add the homes and their details.

Diane Tincher got a huge discount on her home in rural Japan

Teru Oshima, the founder of Oshimaland, says that in the past such properties were "not very attractive options" in Japan.

Yet as house prices have surged in the country in recent years, as well as a big increase in the wider cost of living, one 2025 survey found that more than half of Japanese people would now rent a jiko bukken if it was cheap., external

Back in Setagaya, Ryua says she is happy living with her ghostly flatmate. "I believe that ghosts were once living human beings, so they deserve our respect," she says.

#166. Fanatics takes bigger NHL design role with all-32 team Hometown Remix jerseys

Sign up today for the CNBC Sport Newsletter

In most professional sports arenas, rinks and stadiums across the globe, the gear that fans are now wearing to games is made and designed by sports merchandise giant Fanatics. But that isn't always the case for the jersey that the players are wearing on the field, ice or court.

On Monday, Fanatics and the NHL debuted the Hometown Remix program, a collection of uniforms and apparel for all 32 NHL teams. The new designs and look of each jersey is focused on each team's city and fan base. It marks the first time that Fanatics has designed and developed a league-wide jersey program for one of its leagues partners, which include the NFL, NBA, and MLB.

For some leagues, such as the NFL, while Fanatics may manufacture jerseys that fans buy in stores, other consumer-focused products and even what coaches wear on the sidelines, what players wear on the field may be manufactured and designed by a different company. In the case of the NFL, that is Nike. In MLB, while Fanatics manufactures player jerseys, Nike designs them.

Fanatics has been a partner of the NHL since 2005, mainly focused on retail and e-commerce. But in 2023, as the league's jersey deal with Adidas lapsed, Fanatics expanded its partnership to include on-ice uniforms and authentic jerseys.

When that deal began alongside the 2024-25 season, it marked the first time that Fanatics branding appeared directly on an official player jersey in a professional game, a massive evolution for the company.

The new collection takes that a "giant step forward in our partnership with the NHL," according to Andrew Low Ah Kee, CEO of Fanatics Commerce, the company's merchandise business arm.

"What's exciting to me about it is that not only is it all 32 teams, it's actually that Fanatics is singularly responsible and accountable for the design and execution of the program in partnership with the [NHL] and the clubs," Low Ah Kee said. "Whereas we work with Nike and others on other leagues and different programs."

Low Ah Kee said the process for this collection began when its expanded deal with the NHL took effect in 2024, and Fanatics' design team has been working across the league, its teams and players since.

Some of the process itself hasn't been totally new: Fanatics has designed jerseys in conjunction with the NHL and some of its teams for tentpole events like the Winter Classic, Stadium Series and the 4 Nations Face-Off. However, not every NHL team has participated in one of those events.

But Low Ah Kee said doing it in the NHL, which he said has the "most complex construction of any sports jersey out there," while also adding new design elements, highlighting unique aspects of each team's fans and local culture, has been a big moment for Fanatics.

"I don't think this will be a capstone, but this is certainly a big milestone in the relationship that we have," Low Ah Kee said.

Brian Jennings, NHL senior vice president and chief partnerships and club strategy officer, said the league appreciates the legacy and reverence that hockey jerseys — often called sweaters — hold for fans of the sport. At the same time, Jennings said the league has been excited to create new designs around a league-wide platform that celebrates local team culture.

"Each market and city is unique … we love to celebrate that uniqueness," Jennings said.

Jennings said that each team will wear its jersey for a set of games throughout the remainder of this season, providing opportunities to highlight the designs and storytelling.

While this is just set to be a program for the 2026-2027 season, Jennings said that if a team wanted to integrate some of the logos, colors or designs into a future alternate jersey or other products, the NHL and Fanatics would be open to it.

This Hometown Remix collection is also setting the groundwork for future league-wide efforts with Fanatics, Jennings said. "In an ideal world, we'll start to ideate immediately around what is another version," he said. "Once we have the ability to collect some insights, what was successful, what wasn't, what do we want to turn the volume up on … we want to keep the design integrity really, really high and delight and excite fans."

Jennings said these moments do boost the business side as well. While he declined to provide specific targets, he pointed to the bump that the league and teams see when new jerseys and merchandise are released for special event games like the Winter Classic. He also noted the follow-on effects on things like ticket sales and sponsorship activation when teams host special theme nights or special jersey matchups. "It creates a lot of excitement for the clubs that they can market around," he said.

Low Ah Kee said Fanatics is "very aware" that the launch of this league-wide program is happening just ahead of holiday shopping season. At the same time, this collection of jerseys is "limited supply," he said. The prices of these jerseys do vary slightly from some of the traditional NHL jerseys due to some of the unique production features, Fanatics said.

Pricing on Hometown Remix jerseys with custom lettering and numbering are $275 for a premium jersey and $500 for authentics (same as worn on ice by players).

Both Jennings and Low Ah Kee acknowledged the challenges that come with launching new designs of beloved team jerseys: the somewhat unpredictable fan response.

Jennings said that there are so many unique design elements, not just on the front of the jersey but down to the hockey socks that the players wear and how certain parts of the jersey are embossed and raised, that he believes that "fans will really respect that fact."

"The debate about the design will always be ongoing, but I think they'll see the quality come shining through," Jennings said.

When these new jerseys hit the ice on players' backs, it'll come with the Fanatics flag brand on it as well, and Low Ah Kee said while that is a point of pride for the company, it's also a reflection that Fanatics is "accountable for the quality of the garments, the manufacturing of the garments, the design of the garments," he said.

"The protagonists in the story are the fan and their team; we're the supporting actor, and we know that," Low Ah Kee said. "Our job is to think about how do you make that better. Hometown Remix we hope is going to be a really great way for fans to express their love of their cities, their clubs and their markets overall, and we hope people have fun."

#165. Restaurant Brands reports mixed results as Burger King's U.S. turnaround shows signs of success

Restaurant Brands International on Thursday reported mixed quarterly results as Popeyes' struggles offset Burger King's successful U.S. turnaround.

Shares of the company fell about 2% in morning trading.

Here's what the company reported compared with what Wall Street was expecting, based on a survey of analysts by LSEG:

Restaurant Brands reported third-quarter net income attributable to common shareholders of $542 million, or $1.54 per share, up from $315 million, or 96 cents per share, a year earlier.

Excluding certain noncash items, the company earned $1.10 per share.

Across all of its restaurants, the company reported same-store sales growth of 3.8%. Restaurant Brands has reported same-store sales growth above 3% for the past five quarters.

While the broader industry saw traffic soften in the third quarter as gas prices stayed high, Restaurant Brands said it has not seen any significant changes in consumer behavior.

"The environment, from what we see, has been relatively stable over the last couple of quarters and even into recent months," Restaurant Brands CEO Josh Kobza told CNBC.

Burger King was the top performer, with U.S. same-store sales growth of 8.4%. The burger chain's home market is in the middle of multiyear turnaround, with a focus on improving its food and restaurants.

"The plans we've put in place over the last several years are working, and we're going to keep executing against them," Restaurant Brands Chair Patrick Doyle said on the company's earnings conference call. "Burger King has delivered over four years of industry outperformance, and now we've seen three consecutive quarters of truly exceptional results."

After improving the quality of its Whopper earlier this year, Burger King turned its attention to its nuggets and sauces.

"Nuggets and sauces, frankly, we knew for a while that there was an opportunity," Kobza told CNBC. "We've heard it loud and clear from our guests."

Since Burger King relaunched its nuggets on Sept. 1, with 100% white meat chicken, sales have shot up more than 35%, Kobza said on the earnings call. More core menu items will get an upgrade next year.

Tim Hortons reported same-store sales growth of 1.8% in Canada, its home market. In July, the coffee chain launched a line of matcha drinks, bringing more customers to its locations during the sleepier afternoon hours.

Popeyes was once again the laggard of Restaurant Brands' portfolio. The fried chicken chain's U.S. same-store sales fell 2.2%.

In recent quarters, Popeyes has struggled as other chicken rivals have lured its customers away with discounts. Restaurant Brands is trying to stage a comeback with improved restaurant operations and a focus on its core menu.

Restaurant Brands previously forecast that Popeyes would return to same-store sales growth in the second half of 2026, but Kobza said on the earnings call that the company now believes it will take longer, although traffic is improving. The return of Popeyes' $6 Big Box promotion in September boosted sales less than expected, according to Kobza. Plus, the chain has scaled back its wings to refocus on its core menu items, hurting sales in the short term.

The company's international business saw same-store sales growth of 3.9%, fueled by its Burger King locations.

#164. How video game remakes tap the power of nostalgia

The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time is often rated as one of the greatest video games

The moment she saw footage from the remake of The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time, Kacey Doran picked up her phone. She had to tell her sister.

"Have you seen this?" messaged Doran, excitedly.

Nearly 30 years ago, a 10-year-old Doran had sat in front of her family's TV, reading out the dialogue on screen to her sister, a year younger, as they inched their way through the original Nintendo 64 game's sprawling, puzzle-filled dungeons. Ocarina's fantasy-like story, and beautiful music, enthralled them both.

It was also the first game Doran ever completed. Now, countless memories rushed back to her as she devoured clips of the fully redesigned version, due to arrive on the Switch 2 later this year.

Doran's sister messaged back: "I'm not tearing upâ¦ YOU'RE tearing up."

News of a video game remake bought back happy memories for Kacey Doran

This is the power of nostalgia â and a perfect example of how video games can trigger it. Doran now teaches an esports course at Rowan University in New Jersey. She says the original Ocarina of Time, released in 1998, had a "magical" quality that she hopes to rediscover, and also share with her sister's children.

The video games industry has long leaned on remakes. From Mario Kart to the Final Fantasy series, or even Pac-Man, some games just keep coming back in updated forms, as new generations of gamers emerge and as computer hardware improves, enabling more detailed graphics and increasingly expansive game worlds.

While the emotional hook may be the most significant draw, putting out a remake can be a double-edged sword. Some gamers are very protective of their favourite titles and new versions don't always meet certain fans' astronomically high expectations.

Nostalgia is an important but mixed emotion, says Tim Wulf, at the Centre for Advanced Internet Studies in Bochum, Germany. It has positive aspects but also a kind of sadness, too. Happiness in remembering things that were important to us, and also an awareness that that time is now gone.

"It's a social emotion because memories that induce nostalgia for us are connected to other people," he explains, adding that nostalgia can boost our self-esteem while highlighting key social bonds.

Video games, says Wulf, are notoriously good at triggering nostalgia because gamers can "jump into the same environment". In other words, games are like time machines for our emotions, and returning to fondly remembered places can feel stabilising or reassuring in an ever-changing world.

Doran, for one, notes that when you return to a favourite old game, there's something satisfying about deploying previously learned skills. You know what to do here, you're winning again. Just like the old days.

Video games are good at triggering nostalgic feelings says Tim Wulf

Putting out remasters and remakes has been a "really successful" strategy for the games industry, says Piers Harding-Rolls, head of games research at Ampere Analysis.

"Tying in with established audiences and building on existing content is quite a safe route for a lot of companies," he adds. In many cases, it is cheaper to update an existing game rather than create a new title from scratch.

Among other remakes that have found big audiences recently are those of Resident Evil 4 and Assassin's Creed Black Flag.

But Ocarina of Time perhaps stands out because, ever since its original release, it has been considered by many as one of the greatest games ever made. "The interest from all the Gen Zs that I work with is really high," says Harding-Rolls. The legend of The Legend of Zelda, as it were.

Since 2007 there have been 14 main releases of Assassin's Creed

Clearly, nostalgia is not the only driver of interest in the remake, and today's audiences will expect a game that plays to today's standards.

Games companies must keep innovating when linking contemporary audiences to the past, says Wulf, in order satisfy their desire for fresh experiences. And although Wulf himself was a big fan of the original, he has no personal interest in the Ocarina of Time remake. "I want a new game â I don't want to play an old game," he says. Nostalgia isn't always enough for some.

Plus, critics often highlight a tension between originals and remakes. Which is the definitive version? In 2021, Square Enix released the bizarrely titled Nier Replicant ver.1.22474487139... â a remaster of a game first published in 2010. One reviewer wrote, external, "What we're left with is two nearly identical Niers each vying to be the true Nier."

Fans may react badly should a remaster or remake fail to do justice to the source material. And, in the gaming world, angry fan reactions can be serious.

"There's always going to be that fan base or group of people that are going to nit-pick," says Stephanie Ijoma, chief executive and founder of NNESAGA, a consultancy and creative agency.

Ultimately, games developers working on remakes have to "find the right balance" between appealing to fans and allowing themselves to be creative with the material, she adds. "You can't make art in fear, you can't create in fear."

Ijoma gives the example of Final Fantasy VII. First released in 1997, it got a highly anticipated remake in 2020.

This was a game Ijoma played back in the late 1990s and she was thrilled to be able to share it with younger players today.

First released in 1997, Final Fantasy VII got a remake in 2020

Remakes enable this sharing of experiences because getting old computer hardware or video games consoles to work, while a pastime loved by some, is not possible for everyone.

"I've tried multiple times to play Silent Hill 2 because I'm interested in older games but it's such a difficult game to come into nowadays," says Niklas Hallin, a solo indie game developer who runs the studio Neckbolt. "They made a remake and it's so smooth, it just works. It works on your modern hardware."

Hallin did not play many games as a child so he says nostalgia isn't much of a motivator for him. However, in his own games, he likes to include subtle stylistic references to classic games because he knows that this appeals to gamers.

Indie developers often lean on retro games aesthetics not just because people respond well to them â but also because they are easier to achieve. "You can't compete with billion dollar budgets, super high-fidelity, Triple A graphics," he says.

One question remains. The visual difference, today, between games released years apart is, arguably, negligible. So will there be any point in remaking a game from 2026 in, say, 2038? Will technology have moved on so much by then that it's actually worth it?

Harding-Rolls predicts that games companies will still seek to connect new audiences with classic titles by offering updates.

Remakes might become less about graphical improvements and more about editorial adjustments. "Reinterpretations," he says. "Or versions with additional content."

X-planes: Are they needed in the new era of drones?

The start-ups hoping to return battery making to the US

#162. Crypto giant Binance hit by $6.3bn hack suspected to be largest in history

Cryptocurrency giant Binance said on Thursday that $6.3bn (Â£4.7bn) of digital assets have been impacted by what is thought to be the biggest crypto hack on record.

The world's biggest crypto exchange said about half of those assets had been frozen but that many remained in the hands of the hackers, adding that it will "cover all losses".

The firm said while initial checks suggested an engineer's laptop had been compromised, allowing an attacker to make unauthorised withdrawals, "our subsequent investigation has uncovered additional points of breach".

Two analytics firms have attributed the incident, which eclipses a record-setting $1.5bn hack on the Bybit exchange in 2025, to a North Korean hacking unit called TraderTraitor.

Cyber-security firm Check Point said the unit is also known in the industry as Lazarus Group, which has been blamed for the 2025 hack.

Check Point's chief information security officer Jayant Dave said the losses estimated by Binance made it "the largest crypto theft on record".

AI-powered investigations company TRM Labs said in a report that several elements of the Binance attack resemble earlier North Korean-linked incidents, including the Bybit hack.

The lab's global head of policy Ari Redbord told the BBC the Binance hack is a "watershed moment" that is unlike anything the industry has seen.

The attacker still holds an enormous sum of stolen assets, meaning efforts to trace and recover the funds are far from over, Redbord added.

Binance, which was founded in China in 2017, allows users to trade digital assets and is estimated to have more than 300 million registered users worldwide.

The company said in 2024 that it has more than $160bn in assets under its custody.

The exchange has been subject of regulatory scrutiny around the world.

Chainalysis, a crypto analytics firm, also said it has "high confidence" that the North Korean group are linked to the hack.

For the last few years North Korea-linked operatives have focussed on attacking cryptocurrency companies for large thefts of digital tokens.

Western security agencies have previously said stolen funds are used to finance North Korea's nuclear weapons and missile development programs.

The regime has denied any involvement in hacks in the past.

Eric Jardine, Chainalysis' Head of Research, urged every exchange to remain vigilant as the platforms are attractive targets for hackers due to the sheer value of assets.

Binance did not directly respond to BBC questions about the incident's potential links to North Korea but said: "We are working closely with our law enforcement partners to confirm the identity of these criminals and bring them to justice."

The incident concerns Binance.com - while much smaller crypto platform Binance.US, which provides trading services specifically for American residents - said its platform is not affected by the issue.

Binance co-CEO Richard Teng said the company's initial investigation suggested the hack started with a compromised engineer's laptop.

"That gave an attacker access to a service container, where they submitted unauthorized withdrawal transactions," he said in a statement.

"To be clear: the attacker did not simply use one engineer's permissions, and this was not a single 'one-click' action. It involved a more complex attack process. Our investigation has not attributed responsibility to any individual. So far, we have found no evidence of any wallet private key leak."

He said a detailed "post-mortem" would be shared once the investigation was complete.

In an update, the company said in a statement on X that another investigation found there had been other breach points: "We are still gathering the facts, and will share more directly as we have them."

Check Point's Dave said this attack also resembled a hack on crypto exchange platform Bitget in September in which $388m (Â£289m) was lost: "This implies that attackers no longer need the vault keys. They target the systems and people with authority to use them."

In a series of posts, Yi He, Binance's co-chief executive, thanked various cryptocurrency issuers for freezing the assets.

"Safety first: withdrawals are currently disabled until the environment is confirmed to be secure," she said.

Two other major crypto platforms, Tether and Circle, said they had frozen a total of about $2.4bn of assets in response to the potential Binance hack.

World Liberty Financial, a crypto company linked to US President Donald Trump and his family, said it has worked with its partners to freeze stolen funds.

Crypto exchange Coinbase said it was unaffected by the incident and was monitoring the situation.

Several crypto industry observers had flagged concerns about a potential hack on Binance hours before the exchange confirmed the security issue.

Bitcoin dropped by more than 4% following the alerts. The price of other major cryptocurrencies including Ethereum and Solana also fell sharply.

#161. Fed will vote on rates less often. What that could mean for borrowers

After the Federal Reserve held its benchmark interest rate steady last week, Fed Chairman Kevin Warsh announced plans to reduce the number of rate-setting votes over the next two years. That move could potentially extend the timeline for everyday Americans to see borrowing costs adjust, some experts say.

"Fewer Fed meetings in 2027 could mean consumers wait longer to see relief when borrowing costs are elevated," said James Vrachas, executive director at advisory firm Post Oak Group.

Americans may feel somewhat removed from the Fed, but the central bank's moves have a ripple effect on many types of consumer products, including the borrowing rates for credit cards, home loans and auto financing.

For example, most credit cards have variable rates tied to the prime rate, which moves with the Fed's benchmark. However, even with the central bank holding rates unchanged, credit card rates are edging higher as issuers protect themselves from risk, according to LendingTree.

Card issuers are mitigating their exposure from borrowers who may fall behind on payments or default in the face of stubborn inflation in everyday expenses, said Matt Schulz, LendingTree's chief credit analyst.

Generally, card issuers are slow to bring rates down, unless prompted by the Fed's actions, said Schulz: "Issuers will tinker with rates a bit at the margins at other times, but the big moves tend to only come when issuers are pushed."

On Oct. 28, the central bank voted to keep the benchmark borrowing rate at the current target range of 3.75% to 4% as policymakers deliberated next steps with inflation remaining well above the Fed's 2% goal.

The Fed will keep meeting eight times a year, Warsh said at the news conference following the October meeting, but next year, one of the eight meetings will not entail a vote on interest rates or include a post-meeting statement. The "strategy" meetings will increase to two the following year, leaving just six opportunities to adjust rates — with the exception of emergency meetings, if necessary.

"For consumers, that could mean longer periods of uncertainty around borrowing costs," said Jordan Ricciardi, a senior wealth strategy associate at UBS. "Our clients looking to buy a home, refinance a mortgage or finance a business could be impacted as fewer scheduled opportunities means the Fed isn't as quickly responding to changing economic conditions."

With fewer scheduled opportunities to adjust rates, that means borrowing rates could remain stubbornly high even if economic conditions begin to improve, Post Oak Group's Vrachas also said.

As it stands, financial institutions can adjust rates whenever market conditions warrant, regardless of the Fed's meeting schedule, he said. In the meantime, though, many consumers may continue to face elevated borrowing costs for mortgages, car loans and credit card debt, among other financial products.

"For consumers, the bigger concern isn't necessarily fewer meetings, but whether lenders pass along lower borrowing costs as quickly as they pass along increases," Vrachas said.

Consumers may be even more impacted by the lack of transparency, economists say, as Warsh implements measures to curtail so-called forward guidance, or how the Fed signals its future rate moves.

Although economic conditions have become more complicated and information is more widely available, "Warsh keeps his proverbial cards closer to his chest than his recent predecessors have," said Mark Hamrick, an economic analyst and founder of The Hamrick Brief, a weekly newsletter.

"I wouldn't expect consumers to see the universe of market rates change simply because the Fed votes less often," he said. "But the public may have less opportunity to hear from the source about how and why the Fed is making the decisions that shape what they pay to borrow and what they earn on their savings."

In the interim, "the best protection for consumers remains shopping around, particularly for the highest yields among savings accounts and [certificates of deposit]," Hamrick said.

As long as the Fed keeps rates unchanged, some top-yielding online savings accounts are offering interest rates as high as 4% or more, Bankrate data shows. "That's one of the upsides of an elevated interest rate environment: savers can earn meaningfully higher yields," Hamrick said.

#159. Faisal Islam: This Budget was micro and that was deliberate

It was not a mini Budget, but it was rather small. It might be called the "micro Budget".

Small does not necessarily mean bad, but it clearly falls short of radical action to create a promised new economic model.

A new "theory of growth" was not unveiled as promised by the prime minister Andy Burnham.

It was preamble, roadmap, consultation, perhaps the starter to the main course, which appears to be the PM's 10-year plan next month.

It went unsaid, but the clear stategic aim of Healey's statement was to be the "anti-mini Budget".

This "steady as she goes" predictable fiscal event set out to avoid the aftermath seen following Liz Truss's mini-budget, in which unexpected and unfunded tax cuts spooked the bond and currency markets.

The markets didn't even flicker on Healey's announcement of Â£65bn of extra borrowing over the next five years - government interest costs have actually fallen since Wednesday, against the backdrop of market turmoil due to the US-Iran war.

The core of the Budget was to get Downing Street through the impacts of the Middle East conflict shock on energy and borrowing costs.

If these pressures prove enduring there are some unpalatable decisions that will have to be grasped within the next year. The can will have to be picked up rather than kicked down the road again.

It buys more time, more "breathing space", as it were, for the government and the prime minister's long-term strategic plans.

The detail may come more quickly than feared. On welfare, the Milburn Review process is clearly heading for some sort of welfare-to-work scheme, even a separate system for younger workers.

It will cost money up front to subsidise work opportunities and hope to save more from welfare bills in the future. The chancellor saw at close quarters Gordon Brown do this twice two decades ago.

The Office for Budget Responsbility's (OBR) economic forecast showed the sheer rise in the welfare bill, which is set to jump to Â£431bn in 2031, if pensioner support is included.

There are two further areas where the chancellor laid down markers, with little fiscal cost, in terms of the long term plan.

On energy, there is some significance to the "cheaper power" mission announced, reshaping what had previously been a focus on clean power.

While still committed to Net Zero, a series of technical changes to powers over levies and renewable auctions, will seek to turn the tide on additional pressures on energy costs.

On devolution, the plan to allow local mayors to keep some extra business rate and income tax revenues, are more radical than expected.

There are trade offs here, but it essentially provides incentives for regional leaders to grow their economies, workforce and tax base, that are normal elsewhere, but have not existed in the UK's very centralised tax system.

How the PM will square other aspects of his "theory of growth" with the chancellor's spending constraints is the other significant challenge.

Can he announce a 10-year plan without detailing the Liverpool to Manchester high speed train line, or a rebirth of some cancelled HS2 lines he championed a year ago?

Away from the long term, the OBR forecast point to a very tricky economic backdrop until the spring, and a trickier spending review, given the need to accommodate the new defence target of 3% of national income by 2030.

The PM's insistence he will not call an election for a year are eminently believable given the risk of 5% inflation and declining household income.

A "focused" Budget at an uncertain time achieved its narrow aim of a bit of "breathing space" for households and also for the government itself.

But the evident gaps in the Budget will need filling in come the PM's upcoming 10-year plan.

#157. Defense, healthcare, crypto and AI: How the midterm election could affect stocks

Lockheed Martin gets about 70% of its sales from the U.S. government. Medicaid accounts for three-quarters of Molina Healthcare's premium revenue. Coinbase is waiting on Congress to finish writing the rules for cryptocurrency.

For some of the biggest U.S. companies, Washington is part of the business model. Now, the midterm election could change who holds the leverage.

A Democratic victory in either or both chambers of Congress would grant the party new leverage to investigate companies, challenge federal spending and demand legislative concessions. But their reach would have limits: President Donald Trump would retain his veto and broad authority over tariffs, immigration and federal regulation.

Historically, stocks have performed well under divided government. Under Republican presidents, the S&P 500 has gained an average of 23% in the year after midterms when Congress was split, compared with 16% when Democrats controlled both chambers, according to a Morgan Stanley analysis published Thursday. Analysts often attribute the pattern in part to congressional gridlock limiting sweeping policy changes.

Still, some analysts caution against assuming that pattern will repeat.

"We don't think that blueprint holds up this time around, and we still see plenty of reasons to be constructive on the U.S. equity market," said Ariana Salvatore, head of U.S. public policy research at Morgan Stanley.

For investors, the midterm election could reshape which businesses preserve their advantages in Washington, particularly in industries such as defense, healthcare, banking and artificial intelligence.

"There are some policy changes that might be market moving, at least for certain segments," said Jeff Buchbinder, chief equity strategist at LPL Financial.

Control of either the House or Senate would give Democrats committee gavels and subpoena power, allowing them to demand documents, compel testimony and haul executives before public hearings.

On Oct. 21, Sen. Richard Blumenthal, D-Conn., the top Democrat on the Senate Homeland Security and Governmental Affairs Permanent Subcommittee on Investigations, sought records tied to Trump's White House ballroom from donors including Cisco, Salesforce, ServiceNow and Kraken. Apple and Nvidia each committed $25 million to the project, according to records obtained by The Washington Post.

Rep. Jamie Raskin, D-Md., has separately sought records involving tariff exclusions, including communications involving Apple's former CEO, Tim Cook, as Democrats investigate whether Trump used trade policy to reward political allies and donors.

While donations do not prove wrongdoing or favorable treatment, congressional probes still carry risks: subpoenas consume executive bandwidth, reveal internal communications and generate unpredictable news coverage. Few CEOs relish the idea of sitting at a witness table before lawmakers and television cameras.

"It creates volatility because you get a negative headline," said Monica Guerra, Morgan Stanley's head of policy and geopolitical strategy, who expects Democrats to cast a wide net for their probes.

Democrats have signaled interest in Trump family businesses, government contractors, firms tied to Donald Trump Jr.'s 1789 Capital, and companies associated with Elon Musk and the so-called Department of Government Efficiency.

Then there is the most basic power Congress has: deciding where the money goes.

Trump's 2025 tax cuts and spending law, known as One Big Beautiful Bill, is projected to reduce federal Medicaid spending by $911 billion through 2034, according to a KFF analysis of Congressional Budget Office estimates. KFF is a nonpartisan nonprofit that provides health policy information. Meanwhile, new work requirements for Medicaid enrollees are scheduled to take effect Jan. 1, or earlier in some states, while tighter state budgets could mean fewer people are covered and greater pressure on provider payments.

Molina Healthcare generated $32.2 billion, or 75% of its 2025 premium revenue, from Medicaid, according to the company's annual Securities and Exchange Commission filing. In the same filing, Molina warned that losing government contracts or failing to renew them on favorable terms could materially reduce premium revenue.

A Democratic-controlled chamber could not repeal the 2025 law on its own but could use must-pass budget negotiations to seek funding changes or delays to some cuts.

"They're probably going to push back on those Medicaid cuts," Buchbinder told CNBC, noting that preserving coverage could help stabilize enrollment for Medicaid insurers and patient volumes for hospitals.

Hospitals face their own squeeze: fewer insured patients can mean more uncompensated care, while state budget pressure can reduce reimbursement rates, according to KFF. Congress created a $50 billion rural health fund over five years to cushion some of the blow, but KFF and medical providers have said the money would not fully offset the broader Medicaid reductions.

Democratic gains could still cut both ways for healthcare. The same lawmakers seeking to protect Medicaid funding could also intensify scrutiny of insurers, hospitals and drugmakers over prices, coverage and profits.

Defense contractors face a fight over which weapons and programs Congress keeps funding. The White House has requested $1.5 trillion for national defense in fiscal 2027, with a focus on replenishing weapons, bolstering readiness and covering other needs, some tied to the Iran war.

The White House is separately seeking an $87.6 billion supplemental appropriation from Congress for fiscal 2026, driven primarily by military operations related to Iran.

A Democratic Congress could challenge some Iran- and Israel-related spending while pushing for more aid to Ukraine. However, bipartisan consensus on China deterrence, nuclear modernization and missile defense could protect other major programs, according to multiple analysts.

For contractors, the difference could mean billions of dollars in orders.

Lockheed Martin, for example, recently reached a framework agreement with the Pentagon covering 850 F-35 fighter jets over a decade. But the agreement is not yet a finalized contract: Congress must authorize the multiyear purchase and provide the funding.

"You want to be a stock picker and not just ride the whole sector," Guerra told CNBC, pointing to areas such as shipbuilding, autonomous systems and missile defense as likely winners.

For crypto companies, the midterms could determine whether Trump's regulatory shift becomes durable law or remains vulnerable to the next administration. The industry's push for permanence stalled in September, when the Senate failed to advance the Trump-backed Clarity Act, a sweeping market structure bill.

Shares of Coinbase and stablecoin issuer Circle fell as much as 11% when the measure failed.

Getting a bill through the next Congress could come at a price. Democratic gains would give the party more leverage to demand stricter consumer safeguards, limits on yield-bearing stablecoins and tighter scrutiny of Trump's crypto businesses.

"In some ways, the industry doesn't really care who got elected," said Sam Stovall, chief investment strategist at CFRA Research. "They just want to know what the rules are going to be for the coming year."

Federal regulators estimate their proposed capital overhaul would cut requirements for the largest banks by roughly 4.8%, potentially freeing billions for lending and shareholder payouts. The Federal Reserve writes the rules, but Senate control shapes who fills future vacancies, and congressional committees can pressure regulators through hearings and investigations.

"It's at the regulatory level where stuff can happen that matters," Buchbinder said.

AI may be the messiest political trade of all.

Trump has made rapid data center construction an economic and national security priority. Morgan Stanley expects hyperscalers including Microsoft, Amazon, Alphabet and Meta to spend more than $1 trillion on capital projects in 2027, even as the buildout faces growing backlash over electricity bills, water use and infrastructure costs.

CNBC's All-America Economic Survey from October found that 63% of registered voters oppose having a data center within 10 miles of their home, compared with just 13% who support one. The level of opposition is higher from Democrats than Republicans.

That friction has already reached Congress. In September, the House voted 417-3 to require state regulators to consider making large data centers cover the additional electricity infrastructure costs needed to serve them. The measure stalled in the Senate, 57-43 on a procedural vote, after Democrats argued it was too weak and pushed for mandatory protections instead of trusting the industry to police itself.

The midterms therefore could decide who gets the power. Democratic gains could put more grid-upgrade costs on tech developers, raising project costs just as access to power is constraining new construction.

But Congress is only part of the equation. State regulators, utilities and local governments will still decide where projects connect to the grid and which facilities get built.

"We think the election can have meaningful consequences, but on a highly localized basis," Salvatore said.

#156. Google value plunges by $500bn as its own AI search hits ad sales

Google CEO Sundar Pichai has backed the company's massive spending on AI.

Google-owner Alphabet lost just over $500bn (Â£374bn) in value on Thursday after it revealed its own artificial intelligence (AI) summaries were hitting search advertising revenue.

Chief business officer Philipp Schindler said Google's "AI mode", where it answers questions conversationally, was eating into the money it makes from ads which appear next to results to traditional search questions.

Yet, Schindler said he felt "very good about our trajectory" with an AI-focused version of the search engine.

Google's role in answering the world's questions has been challenged by the AI boom, with millions of people using programs such as ChatGPT and Claude instead.

Kim Scott, who formerly led Google's advertising business, told the BBC's Today programme that the company would "rather eat its own lunch than allow someone else to eat their lunch" by having its own AI summaries hit its search revenue.

She said that Google was founded on the idea of making long-term bets on technology â even if they hurt the firm in the short term.

"I think they're making a long-term bet on AI," she said.

In its results published on Wednesday, Alphabet revealed that total revenue grew 25% in the three months to the end of September, compared to the same period last year, hitting $127.6bn.

However, the firm did not make as much money as analysts had predicted, particularly in its advertising arm, which spooked many investors.

Shares in the company plummeted over 12% to a six-month low following the release of the results.

By the end of Thursday, the company's total stock market value had fallen to $3.46 trillion from $3.97 trillion the day before.

Tracy Woo, a principal analyst at Forrester, said Alphabet's disappointing Search business results "highlights the challenge of funding massive AI investments while maintaining the strength of established businesses."

Microsoft and Amazon also issued updates this week, and joined Google in plans to keep spending huge sums of cash in the ongoing AI race.

But investors appeared dismayed at the impacts these plans are having on the ability of each to keep growing their core lines of business.

Amazon's stock took a hit on Thursday , with its shares falling 6% in after-hours trading.

Alongside quarterly sales growth of 11%, hitting $201bn, the company shared a staggering cash flow figure of negative $42bn.

That number shows that Amazon is not only spending more money than it makes, but that spending has increased six-fold over the span of three months.

Chief executive Andy Jassy claimed the business was spending in part because it had the chance at, not one, but two businesses that could end up bringing in $1 trillion for Amazon the future.

Jassy noted that Amazon's cloud services business, as well as retail stores, may be those businesses. During a call with financial analysts he also repeatedly mentioned the financial opportunity presented by Amazon making and selling its own computer chips, as well as its AI developer platform Bedrock, which offers paying users access to most major AI models and tools.

"One of the lessons we've learned over 20-plus years is that there is not one tool to rule the world," Jassy said. "People always assume whatever is in the lead early on will be the only thing that people use - it's never true."

As Jassy was asked several times to explain the company's spending, he admitted it "would be more convenient" if it were spread over the next 25 years. "That's not the world we live in," he added.

"We can fund this plan, and I think it will forever change the size, the revenue, the free cash flow of Amazon."

Wall Street's reaction to Microsoft's update was more muted, with shares falling by less than 1% on Thursday.

The company, which is also one of OpenAI's largest investors, spent around $5bn less on investments dedicated to AI. It also revealed earlier this week plans to cut thousands of workers, which will further reduce the company's costs.

Meanwhile, its revenue grew 19% from a year earlier, hitting $92.3bn for the quarter. Its crucial cloud business grew by 30% to $63bn.

Chief executive Satya Nadella said the use of Copilot, Microsoft's AI chatbot and tool, has seen "automated tasks nearly double since July" as daily users grew by 500% from a year ago.

Usually lower spending and growing sales earns a positive reaction from investors.

But Woo said Wall Street is trying to look farther into the future when it comes to which businesses will truly make good on their big promises around AI.

"Microsoft's results reinforce that enterprise demand for AI remains strong, but they also highlight a much bigger question facing the technology industry, where AI value will ultimately be captured," Woo said.

#155. Burnham 'ready to support' Poundland staff as retailer faces collapse

Andy Burnham has said the government was "ready to support" Poundland staff as the retailer teeters on the brink of collapse.

The prime minister called for answers over why an offer to buy the business from its current owners, which would have protected jobs, had been rejected.

"This is a deeply worrying time for Poundland staff and their families," Burnham told the BBC.

The discount chain is teetering on the edge after its US-based owners Gordon Brothers cut off a vital funding supply and rejected a takeover offer from Irish firm Martin Property Group (MPG).

The uncertainty surrounding Poundland's future has left some of its 12,000 members of staff fearful about the future of their jobs.

Burnham said: "We want to see jobs protected. If there was a potential owner to avoid this that hasn't been accepted, then the business secretary and I want to know why that is the case.

"We stand ready to support the workforce however we can."

The retailer's bosses have filed a notice of intention to appoint administrators, giving the business 10 days to find a rescue deal.

The move comes after managing director Barry Williams told staff that the chain's US owner had cut off a vital supply of funding despite Poundland's strong sales.

It is understood Gordon Brothers wants to break it up, with rivals Poundstretcher, B&M and Lidl interested in potentially buying up its stores.

Business Secretary Jonathan Reynolds earlier said he was "very much concerned" by the uncertainty clouding Poundland's future.

Gordon Brothers bought Poundland, which had been struggling, from the Polish retail group Pepco last year for a nominal Â£1. A little over a year later, it put the business up for sale.

Despite an offer on the table for the business, Poundland's managers and owners have not been able to agree on a buyer.

It could still be purchased out of administration in a so-called pre-pack deal, but the most likely option is for it to be broken up and for Poundland's stores to be sold off.

Poundland was founded in 1990 with a store in Burton-upon-Trent and has around 20 million customers per year.

It has across its 600 stores in the UK and Ireland, where it trades as Dealz.

It sells low-cost everyday products, general merchandise and fashion on local high streets.

The curious case of why Poundland is struggling during a cost-of-living crisis

Poundland in rescue talks as owners seek buyer

A Gordon Brothers insider said breaking the firm up would allow creditors to recover more money and that this process would ultimately offer the best outcome for staff. The US investment firm's position is that current employees may be able to get employment with new store owners.

If this type of insolvency happens, it would let interested parties firm up offers.

These include Poundstretcher owner, Fortress, which according to one insider is interested in taking on between 200 and 250 stores.

Another large retailer is understood to be interested in buying up more than 100 other sites.

More than half a dozen retailers, include Iceland, Lidl, and B&M, have expressed interest in Poundland's store estate.

Bally Auluk, a national officer at the shopworkers' union Usdaw, said this week had been "incredibly distressing" for Poundland staff.

He claimed staff were having to "watch from the sidelines as a multi-billion pound company jeopardises their livelihoods as it tries to extract Â£30m from a business it bought for Â£1".

He said Williams had "reluctantly" kicked off the administration process to give the business time to find a rescue deal, adding that Usdaw was urging Gordon Brothers to reconsider.

Staff chanted "Save our Jobs" outside Poundland's customer support centre in Walsall on Thursday afternoon.

Staff at Poundland's customer support centre in Walsall held a protest on Thursday, with some dressing as the Poundland "Pound Hound" mascot.

One employee said they were trying to "save our jobs".

There is also a split between the owners and the management running Poundland, who described the situation as "unexpected, unwanted and wholly avoidable".

"As you all know, our sales are strong and our recovery is taking root. This business simply does not deserve to go into any kind of administration," the chain's managing director Williams said in a message to staff this week.

He revealed that Gordon Brothers had limited Poundland's access to funds that help it run its day-to-day business.

"I'm disappointed to have to tell you that Gordon Brothers have given instructions that restricts our access to funds that we would, in normal circumstances expect to access fully under our existing loan agreement," he added.

Williams said in the memo, seen by the BBC, that the offer from Martin Property Group was a "fully funded, ready to go bid that would have kept the business intact and able to continue the remarkable progress we've made".

Three independent directors who were appointed earlier this month to help oversee a sale resigned with immediate effect after disagreeing with Gordon Brothers' decision not to accept MPG's solvent offer.

Do you work for Poundland? Share your experience.

#154. Norwegian shares surge 13% on earnings beat, guidance raise

Shares of Norwegian Cruise Line Holdings surged 13% Thursday after the company posted better-than-expected third-quarter earnings and raised its outlook for the full year.

The cruise line reported third-quarter revenue of $2.95 billion, topping Wall Street expectations of $2.88 billion, according to analyst estimates from LSEG.

Net income for the period was $491 million, or $1.07 per share.

Adjusted earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation and amortization was $937 million, which exceeded Norwegian's previous guidance of $874 million. Adjusted EPS was $1.12, exceeding previous guidance of 90 cents and outpacing analysts' expectations of 94 cents, per LSEG.

Norwegian also raised its full-year guidance, citing its stronger-than-expected third-quarter performance. The company now expects 2026 adjusted EBITDA of $2.55 billion and adjusted EPS of about $1.70.

The report said the company's bookings had "improved modestly" since its last report but remain below its optimal range.

Norwegian said it will emphasize new marketing strategies, including the launch of a new marketing platform in January. It also said it anticipates that enhancements such as a new waterpark at its Great Stirrup Cay private island will support stronger demand.

Also on Thursday, Norwegian announced that Chief Financial Officer Mark Kempa will step down after more than 25 years at the company. Meghan Montana, currently chief strategy officer, will take over the role starting Jan. 1.

#151. Lumentum CEO touts 'enormous' opportunity in massive AI connectivity deal with Marvell

Marvell Technology's deal for optical components maker Lumentum will create an "enormous" opportunity to address growing connectivity demands from the artificial intelligence infrastructure buildout, Lumentum CEO Michael Hurlston told CNBC on Monday.

"You need scale-up technology that needs semiconductors and optical working seamlessly together," Marvell CEO Matt Murphy told CNBC's "Squawk on the Street." "We are going to create a new category of company here."

Since the tie-up is a fixed-ratio deal with no cash, the value will depend on the price of Marvell shares. The company said the transaction has an implied enterprise value of $115 billion for Lumentum, in a release.

Marvell shares slumped 13% in trading Monday, while Lumentum's stock sank nearly 19%. But both stocks have been huge winners in the artificial intelligence boom, with Marvell more than doubling in the past year, prior to Monday's sell-off, and Lumentum quadrupling over that stretch.

Through the acquisition, Marvell said it can combine electrical and optical technologies to meet accelerating and shifting AI connectivity demands across regions and systems. Companies are increasingly relying on optical connections to piece together chips used to run and train large language models.

"We're actually going to create solutions around those that are highly sticky, and the reason that they're sold out is because the demand is exploding, and we don't see that changing anytime soon," Murphy said.

Lumentum shareholders will receive 4.5 Marvell shares for each Lumentum share under the agreement. The deal is expected to close during the first half of 2028.

Marvell's takeover marks one of the most significant bets on AI in 2026 and comes despite recent concerns over the long-term sustainability of infrastructure spending.

This isn't the company's first bet on AI growth. This year, the company closed a multibillion-dollar deal for Celestial AI, a startup focused on optical interconnectivity technology.

Marvell's Murphy also shot down concerns about Nvidia's circular dealmaking, given the leading AI chipmaker's recent multibillion-dollar investments in both companies.

In a bid to further support the data center infrastructure that houses its AI chips, Nvidia has positioned itself as a central cog in a network of related companies, investing in several technology companies that purchase its chips or neoclouds that rent out computing capacity. That's sparked criticism that companies like Nvidia are virtually funding themselves by taking stakes in customers.

"This is a double down on supporting Nvidia, who is the ecosystem leader in this area," Murphy told CNBC. "We're very committed to that partnership, and I think having two of the key suppliers come together, that support that ecosystem, is going to be a big benefit for them."

Marvell has seen its market value more than triple over the last three years, buoyed by unrelenting AI enthusiasm. Lumentum's stock has more than doubled in 2026 alone.

CORRECTION: This story has been updated to reflect that Lumentum CEO Michael Hurlston described the deal as an 'enormous' opportunity. A previous version of this story incorrectly cited the Marvell CEO.

#150. Streeting denies 'kicking can down the road' in defence spending row

Wes Streeting has defended the government's current approach

Defence Secretary Wes Streeting has denied "kicking the can down the road" over how much the UK spends on defence.

The government has faced criticism for not setting out a pathway at Wednesday's Budget to hit its target of spending 3% of GDP on defence by 2030.

Streeting is at odds with MPs on the Commons defence committee over the issue.

It comes as Chancellor John Healey and Streeting announce that foreign firms which are awarded defence contracts of more than Â£50m will need to invest an equal value back into the UK.

Under the new investment rules, described as an "offset strategy", these companies will need to spend an equivalent amount on British factories, jobs and training.

The Ministry of Defence will apply the rules on a "case-by-case" basis from April 2027, the government said. Healey and Streeting will unveil the policy on a visit to UK defence contractor BAE's shipbuilding site in Glasgow later.

Countries including Poland, Norway and the Netherlands have mandated similar rules to "boost the economic benefit from defence procurement", the government said.

While ministers hope the rules increase investment in Britain's defence industry, they appear designed to do so in a politically palatable way rather than committing to spending cuts.

Tan Dhesi, Labour chair of the cross-party Commons defence committee, said on Wednesday that decisions had been "kicked into the long grass" to next year's government spending review, rather than announced in the Budget.

He warned that without building capabilities now, "we risk leaving ourselves seriously vulnerable in the event of a major conflict".

Streeting hit back at these comments as "unfair" and "unfounded".

Speaking on Thursday, Dhesi said the committee stands by its criticism.

"We had hoped to see that the chancellor would set out a precise pathway to spend 3% of GDP on defence by 2030," Dhesi told BBC Radio 4's Today programme.

"But instead some of those key decisions on defence have lamentably been delayed until next year's spending review."

He praised the government for plugging a Â£4.7bn hole in the Defence Investment Plan in the Budget, through asking government departments to cut their capital budgets by 1%.

Labour MP Tan Dhesi has led the latest criticism of government defence spending

Responding on Thursday, Streeting told Today: "I like Tan very much, he's a friend and I respect him, but I strongly disagree with him.

"And I don't think the charge he makes of delaying or kicking the can down the road stacks up against the extra money that is already going into defence and the capability we're funding right now."

Streeting told BBC Breakfast the new rules, aiming to create "bang for the British buck", would help achieve this target given increasing defence spending "does come at a cost to the country and other things we'd like to spend money on".

He said waiting for the spending review, which does not have a fixed date and could come in the second half of 2027, was "the right thing to do" so ministers can look at "spending in the round".

He added that the UK was ready to deal with hostile threats, but "we're not where we'd want to be".

"There's no sugarcoating the fact that our Armed Forces were under-invested in for more than a decade," he told Breakfast.

Defence analysts have questioned the government's ability to deliver on its 2030 target and the Nato-wide target of 3.5% by 2035. UK defence spending is at an estimated 2.6% of GDP this year.

Some estimates have suggested the government will need to spend an additional Â£10bn annually to reach its targets.

Tory leader Kemi Badenoch said on Wednesday that Healey "failed his own test" by not setting out a "plan for 3%".

Healey resigned as then-Prime Minister Sir Keir Starmer's defence secretary in June over not spending enough on defence.

In the Budget, Healey said the UK was negotiating with Finland, Poland and the Netherlands to set up an international bank - the Multilateral Defence Mechanism - to boost defence investment and joint procurement.

But Conservative shadow defence secretary James Cartlidge said this amounted to "turning to borrowing at a time when the national debt is over Â£3 trillion".

"The answer is not more borrowing â it's to cut welfare to fund defence," he said.

Healey tells departments to make 1% cuts to fund defence

#149. Merck tops quarterly estimates, hikes profit outlook as new Keytruda form shows strength

Merck on Thursday topped third-quarter estimates and hiked its adjusted profit outlook, as its immunotherapy Keytruda, including a newer injectable form of the drug, showed stronger-than-expected sales.

The pharmaceutical giant now expects adjusted earnings to be between $2.77 and $2.82 per share. That adjusted profit outlook is up from a previous range of $2.66 to $2.76 per share. The guidance now includes a one-time charge of $400 million, or 12 cents per share, related to its licensing agreement with the Chinese cancer drugmaker SciBrunch.

Merck also narrowed the low end of its 2026 revenue guidance, now expecting sales between $66.8 billion and $67.3 billion. That compares to a previous outlook of $66.3 billion to $67.3 billion.

The company has been on a deal spree as it races to offset generic competition for Keytruda after key patents begin expiring in 2028.

Merck is also positioning the new form of the drug, Keytruda Qlex, as a cornerstone of its growth strategy. The goal is to transition patients from the original intravenous version to Qlex, considered a more convenient under-the-skin injection. This strategy allows the company to defend its market share and sustain Keytruda's blockbuster sales beyond the original version's loss of exclusivity.

Keytruda raked in $8.60 billion in sales for the quarter, beating the $7.73 billion that analysts were expecting, according to StreetAccount. That includes $830 million in sales for Qlex – the highest seen in a quarter since it launched in 2025.

Analysts were expecting $649 million in sales, StreetAccount estimates said. The blowout quarter for the shot suggests it could soon eventually be a multibillion-dollar product annually.

Here's what Merck reported for the third quarter compared with what Wall Street was expecting, based on a survey of analysts by LSEG:

The company posted a net income of $5.07 billion, or $2.04 per share, for the quarter. That compares with net income of $5.79 billion, or $2.32 per share, for the year-earlier period.

Excluding acquisition and restructuring costs, Merck posted earnings of $2.49 per share for the third quarter.

Merck raked in $18.09 billion in revenue for the quarter, up 5% from the same period a year earlier.

Beyond Keytruda, Merck's newer products also showed strong growth.

Winrevair, which is used to treat a rare, deadly lung condition, generated $694 million in sales for the quarter, up 93% from the same period a year earlier. Analysts were expecting sales of $645 million.

Meanwhile, the Gardasil vaccine, which prevents cancers from HPV, a very common sexually transmitted infection, drew about $2 billion in sales due to higher demand in the U.S. That tops the $1.76 billion in sales that analysts were expecting.

That strong revenue comes after Merck last year grappled with elevated Gardasil inventory and weak demand for the vaccine in China.

Meanwhile, Merck's animal health business posted $1.68 billion in sales for the third quarter, which came in slightly below analysts' estimates.

#148. Yorkshire Water boss resigns after firm downgraded

Nicola Shaw had been criticised over Yorkshire Water's pay and performance

Yorkshire Water's chief executive has announced she is to step down, just 24 hours after the company was given the lowest possible environmental rating by the Environment Agency.

Nicola Shaw, who has led the firm since 2022, will leave the role on 1 January to be replaced by Nicola Medalova, a chartered environmental health officer who previously worked for the National Grid.

In a statement on Friday, Shaw said it had been a "privilege" to lead the company but that it was the "right time" to leave her post.

Shaw has faced criticism in recent years, including for taking Â£1.9m in undisclosed payments between 2023 and 2026, while the company has come under scrutiny for its performance.

The payments were made by Yorkshire Water's parent company, Kelda Holdings, for what the firm said was "investor engagement" and "financial oversight".

On Thursday, the Environment Agency gave the firm one star out of four - its lowest possible rating - meaning Yorkshire Water was classed as a "poorly performing company".

The EA said Yorkshire Water was responsible for 260 pollution incidents in 2025, which was its highest total since 2011.

In 2024, Ofwat announced it was fining the company Â£47m for historic sewage leaks.

In her statement, Shaw said: "It has been a privilege to lead Yorkshire Water and serve the people of Yorkshire.

"Although there remains a huge amount of work to do, I am extremely proud of what our colleagues have achieved, putting the business on a stronger footing for delivery across all aspects of its work."

Yorkshire Water said Medalova, who is a former non-executive director of the company's board, would join the firm on Monday as part of a transition period before assuming the chief executive officer role full time in the new year.

She said: "I am delighted to be joining Yorkshire Water and grateful to the board for the opportunity to lead the business through its next chapter.

"Yorkshire Water faces huge challenges but also great opportunities for continued improvement and I look forward to working with Nicola Shaw, the leadership team and colleagues across the business as I get up to speed."

In December last year Shaw said "not being transparent" about receiving Â£1.3m in shareholder-funded payments across two instalments had been a "mistake", but had insisted those amounts were "not for me to decide".

She received a further Â£660,000 earlier this year. Ofwat said the payments were not performance-related.

The previously undisclosed payments had come to light the previous August when the government banned Yorkshire Water and five other water companies from paying "unfair" bonuses to bosses.

Tom Gordon, Liberal Democrat MP for Harrogate and Knaresborough, said Shaw should have stepped down "much earlier".

"Good riddance. Nicola Shaw oversaw abysmal management of our precious natural resources, trashing our rivers while receiving an eye-watering salary," he said.

"Her replacement needs to understand that residents are at their wits' end, paying ever-rising bills for polluted rivers and threats of hosepipe bans while someone at the top rakes in millions."

Meanwhile, Judith Cummins, Labour MP for Bradford South, said Yorkshire Water needed "more than a change of leadership" but a "complete change of direction".

"A new face at the top cannot wash away years of Yorkshire Water failing to put customers first," she said.

"Nicola Shaw's time as Yorkshire Water boss saw customers frustrated by poor service, skyrocketing bills, record pollution incidents, and a plummet from 'good' to the worst possible one-star environment rating.

"There must be a wholesale shift in culture that finally prioritises customers. Only then can the company begin to restore public trust."

Robbie Moore, Conservative MP for Keighley and Ilkley, said Shaw's resignation "could not have come quick enough".

He added: "I have therefore already requested an urgent meeting with the new CEO Designate Nicola Medalova to understand how she plans to improve Yorkshire Water's performance at speed.

Chris Stewart at York campaign group Ouse It Lose It said the new CEO's priority had to be looking at "failures in the system".

"There's been a continual decline in the operation of this business," he said.

Stewart said he was not surprised by Shaw's resignation but it was a "shocking indictment of the way the company has been performing, while making loud noises about how much they're putting into improving".

Naomi Tilley, campaigns manager at protest group Surfers Against Sewage, said "systematic change" was needed to fix the company.

"Regardless of who the CEO is, there's sewage entering the sea and the water companies are failing. Yorkshire Water is not an outlier," said Tilley.

"The whole system and every water company is failing in different ways and we are seeing that the system is broken and we need fundamental change.

"While these companies are set up for a profit, we're never going to see pollution curbed and stopped."

Additional reporting by David Rhodes and Grace Wood
