#121. I bonds now pay 5.51% — but there are trade-offs. What to know before buying

Now that Series I bonds pay higher interest, the government-backed, nearly risk-free asset could be more attractive for certain investors. But I bonds have downsides, and there are alternatives to consider.

Newly purchased I bonds currently pay 5.51% annual interest through April 30, up from the 4.26% yield offered on purchases made through October.

Amid rising inflation, the variable part of I bond interest rose to 4.08%, while the fixed part increased to 1.40%, the highest since 2006. Combined, the 5.51% composite rate is the biggest yield since November 2022.

The new I bond rate is "very attractive," according to Ken Tumin, founder of DepositQuest.com, a blog that tracks I bond rates, among other deposit accounts.

The variable part of I bond interest resets every six months, based on the consumer price index. For the next six months after purchase, I bonds look "very competitive" compared to other shorter-term options for cash, such as savings accounts or certificates of deposit, according to Tumin.

Meanwhile, the I bond's 1.4% fixed rate stays the same for as long as you own it, which could appeal to longer-term investors, he said.

But I bonds aren't right for everyone. Here are some key things to know before purchasing.

Investors can buy $10,000 of electronic I bonds per person per calendar year via their TreasuryDirect account. You can open additional TreasuryDirect accounts for other family members or businesses to buy more.

I bond interest is tax-deferred until redemption and is not subject to state or local taxes. You can also avoid federal taxes if you use the money for qualified education expenses.

But experts say there are some downsides. The funds are locked up for 12 months from purchase, and you'll lose your last three months of interest if you redeem within five years.

Advisors typically recommend keeping at least three to six months of living expenses in cash for emergency savings. I bonds could boost those reserves, experts say, as long as you keep enough readily accessible cash elsewhere.

"I always say [I bonds are] a secondary emergency fund," said David Enna, founder of Tipswatch.com, a website that tracks Treasury inflation-protected securities, or TIPS, and I bond rates.

Once you own I bonds for five years, you can redeem the money as needed, penalty-free.

With I bond purchase limits, restrictions and some TreasuryDirect complaints, advisors often recommend other options for clients who want to preserve purchasing power.

"For most of my clients, I bonds are more hassle than they're worth," said certified financial planner Eileen Olson, founder of advisory firm Emerald Wealth in Daufuskie Island, South Carolina. "The amount is too small to matter."

On a $2 million portfolio, the $10,000 annual purchase limit is only 0.5%, and it earns about $550 in a year before federal taxes, she said. "That won't protect anyone's lifestyle from inflation."

Instead, Olson prefers TIPS, which don't have purchase limits and can be bought alongside other assets, outside TreasuryDirect.

TIPS have five-, 10- and 30-year terms, with yields ranging from about 2.3% to above 3% for the 30-year term, as of Nov. 2.

However, the right option depends on your investing goals and timeline, experts say.

"For short-term cash, Treasury bills and high-yield savings are simpler," Olson said.

Also backed by the U.S. government, Treasury bills, or T-bills, have terms ranging from four weeks up to 52 weeks. As of Nov. 2, some T-bill yields were well over 4%.

By comparison, the top 1% average of high-yield savings accounts were paying above 4%, according to rate comparison site DepositAccounts. Some one-year certificates of deposit were also offering rates over 4%, the same day, according to Bankrate.

Money market funds are another option. Some of the biggest funds were paying around 4%, as of Nov. 2, according to Crane Data.

#119. Don't get caught out by new Buy Now Pay Later rules

If you've bought something via Buy Now Pay Later (BNPL) since the summer, then you'll have been tested to see if you can afford it.

BNPL has always been a type of loan, usually paid back in instalments, but it's only been officially regulated since July.

This means stricter background checks on your ability to repay.

If you fail, then your purchase via BNPL will be blocked. If there's a chance of this happening to you, here are some ways to approach the situation.

BNPL lenders - such as Klarna, Clearpay and others - must now have regulator approval to operate, giving shoppers more access to refunds and independent rulings on complaints if things go wrong.

But new affordability checks on each BNPL transaction could also leave you blocked from services for the first time.

There are different affordability checks for different purchases, partially based on how much is borrowed.

But, with the expensive festive season approaching, here's how to avoid being blocked when time and finances are tight.

Here are four things to consider if you find your account is closed or your borrowing limit has been massively reduced, from Sara Williams, a former volunteer debt adviser at Citizens Advice who posts tips as Debt Camel on social media.

Slow down your purchases: is what you are buying essential or can you postpone it?

Be more careful about what you buy to avoid needing a high credit limit to, for example, buy lots of clothes in different sizes

Cancel subscriptions to escape the trap of having to use BNPL to make up for the cost of previous payments

Talk to debt charities for help and access to food bank vouchers if you are using BNPL for essentials

If you are struggling to make repayments on any type of loan - BNPL or otherwise - you should seek help before things spiral out of control.

The government-backed, independent, MoneyHelper website, external has a guide to finding free debt advice near you, external.

It also suggests writing down exactly what you owe, to help plan how you will repay the money.

There are also tools to work out if you are receiving all the benefits and grants you are entitled to, and a bill prioritiser, external to show which payments to make first and what to do if you miss a payment.

You should also be sceptical of supposed debt solutions you might see on your social feeds.

#117. Cheaper cars are selling considerably faster than at the beginning of the year

Consumers are concerned about rising fuel prices and affordability — and that could be showing up in how fast the least expensive cars are moving off dealer lots.

From the first quarter to the third, the number of days it took a dealer to sell a vehicle priced between $20,000 and $29,999 dropped 20%, according to data from Edmunds. That pace of selling saw the biggest acceleration of any price segment.

Vehicles priced from $30,000 to $39,999 sold about 18% faster, and the number of days it took to sell the next tier — $40,000 to $49,999 — dropped nearly 12%, according to Edmunds.

All of those price segments also took fewer days to turn than their more expensive counterparts. The lower-priced tiers took under 60 days to move off lots, with the least expensive ones selling the fastest. Every segment of vehicles above $50,000, meanwhile, took more than 60 days to sell.

"If you're a dealer and you have inventory and that price point, you're definitely noticing," said Edmunds Director of Insights Ivan Drury.

Cheaper vehicles may be selling faster, but they're still hard to find. The average listing price for a new vehicle in September was $49,726, according to Cox Automotive.

And, the overall sales numbers for some key segments priced under $40,000 have fallen. This has especially been true for compact cars, which are down roughly 4% since the beginning of 2026, and compact SUVs, down nearly 8% in the same period, according to Cox Automotive.

These different data points seem to be moving in opposite directions. On the one hand, cheaper cars are moving faster off dealer lots. On the other, sales of affordable segments are down.

There are a few reasons for this, according to analysts who follow the industry. One is that there might not be enough lower-priced vehicles on dealer lots in the first place.

"I think there are some supply things going on at that end of the market," said Cox Automotive Deputy Chief Economist Mark Strand. "We have to kind of grade the market on a curve until we see what's going on with fourth-quarter data."

The compact SUV segment, in particular, is suffering from the lack of two popular models.

The Toyota RAV4, which is the most popular SUV in the U.S., has been in short supply at many dealerships this year, as the automaker shifts production to its Kentucky factory, Strand said. The company also has shifted the entire lineup to a hybrid powertrain at a time when hybrids have been in exceptionally high demand.

The other missing SUV is the Ford Escape, which that automaker discontinued to make way for its upcoming midsize EV pickup truck.

The other slow-selling, low-priced segment — compact cars — faces structural inventory challenges, said AutoForecast Solutions Vice President of Global Forecasting Sam Fiorani.

"We've seen the disappearance of affordable, compact new vehicles over the last decade," he said.

Detroit automakers swapped out their lineups of compact coupes and sedans in favor of small crossover SUVs, which tend to carry slightly higher sticker prices than comparably sized passenger cars.

Currently, there is not a single compact passenger sedan for sale by an American manufacturer. Most on the market are Japanese or Korean, with a few German models, such as the Volkswagen Jetta and the Golf hatchback.

"You have all these brands that have just abandoned the marketplace," Fiorani said.

The smallest American passenger cars are the Ford Mustang, General Motors' Chevrolet Corvette, and the midsize Tesla Model 3 sedan, according to AutoForecast Solutions. The firm classifies both the Mustang and the Corvette as sports cars, and as such they occupy a different niche than compact sedans like the Honda Civic or Toyota Corolla.

The Mustang has a starting price of $33,490, the Model 3 starts at $34,990 and the Chevrolet Corvette starts at $71,300. Cars like the Civic, Corolla and Jetta, meanwhile, all start below $25,000.

"Compact vehicle buyers are very price-sensitive," Fiorani said. "And so when the price of gas goes up, that eats into their car payment. That means that they can't afford a $500 or $600 a month car payment, and they have to move into the used market."

Indeed, the wholesale used prices of compact cars have risen and are up more than almost any other segment, according to Cox Automotive. Wholesale prices — the prices vehicles sell for at large auctions, like Cox Automotive's Manheim auction — are indicators for prices overall in the fragmented used-car market.

Along with the rise in prices, auto finance stats show that a historically high number of buyers are stretching to afford cars.

Roughly one in four buyers financing their purchases are getting 84-month loans — a record. Nearly 3 in 10 trade-ins toward new-vehicle purchases carried negative equity in the third quarter, meaning the cars being traded in were worth less than the outstanding balances of the loans on them. Shoppers are often rolling these negative balances into new loans, which pushes up the new payment cost.

Automakers have also been more tightly aligning production with demand to avoid discounting vehicles. Incentives on average are about 6.6% of the average transaction price as of October, according to Cox Automotive. Leasing offers a way to get a car with a lower payment, but leasing rates are also down.

Vehicle trade-in data suggests that many buyers who are snapping up those fast-selling cheaper vehicles are either first-time buyers or are purchasing supplemental vehicles, Edmunds' Drury said.

The subcompact SUV segment is one lower-priced group that has seen sales rise in 2026. That is a segment that attracts a lot of first-time buyers. But this is still a relatively small share of the total car market, Fiorani said.

"It's not the mainstream vehicle that Americans want," he said. "It is the vehicle that entry-level buyers need to get to work, to get to school. ... It's people who don't have families yet."

Repeat buyers are less likely to want to step down into a compact car or SUV, Drury said.

"The moment you get into that other vehicle and it's a little bit smaller, it's not as comfortable, it doesn't fit all your stuff, all your kid's equipment for soccer practice, you're done," he said. "You're not giving up 360 cameras, ventilated seats, heated steering wheels, these niceties that you've become accustomed to. Automakers know that too. Dealers know that."

The truly cash-strapped are either snapping up the relatively few low-priced cars on dealer lots, moving to the used market, or holding on to their cars longer.

"The people who are trying to buy the smaller vehicles are really hurt by the way the economy is at the moment," Fiorani said. "So those people are trying to move into used vehicles or trying to maintain their current vehicle as long as they can. Automakers should be prepared for a softening in the marketplace."

#115. CNBC's The China Connection newsletter: Nvidia's humanoid push relies on China-made robot hands

Hi, this is Evelyn, writing to you from Beijing. Welcome to the latest edition of The China Connection — a snapshot of what I'm seeing and hearing from local businesses.

Chinese robot companies still underpin many U.S. tech endeavors, especially when it comes to the pain point of robotic hands. Who is one of the latest on Nvidia's radar?

In just a few months, Nvidia has showcased two humanoid robot training packages: one with mechanical hands from Sharpa, and another with hands from Wuji.

Pan Yunzhe founded Wuji in 2019, eventually moving much of the team to Shenzhen and drawing backers such as Ant Group, HSG and Future Capital, according to PitchBook. A demo video last week claimed a Wuji Hand 2 could autonomously spin a pen, despite interference.

The Wuji Hand 2 made it onto a slide Oct. 21 behind Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang as he told a crowd in Berlin about a new humanoid hardware system for training the robots on Nvidia software. The robot body comes from Agile Robots, which has offices in Germany, China and the U.S.

"It will be the flywheel by which we can accelerate development of robotics," Huang said of the hardware system, called Agile One SN. Agile Robots announced the system, which uses Nvidia's Isaac robotics software and Jetson Thor computing platform.

Research institutions, such as Stanford and ETH Zurich, can likely start using the Agile One SN system in early 2027, according to Nvidia.

Stanford and ETH Zurich were also among the institutions Nvidia named in connection with its first reference humanoid design, announced May 31. That system uses a body from Chinese humanoid company Unitree and mechanical hands from Sharpa. It was scheduled for delivery from late 2026.

Nvidia did not immediately respond to a request for comment on whether deliveries had begun, or whether the researchers would try both reference robots, or just one.

Sharpa, headquartered in Singapore, was co-founded by David Li, CEO of Shanghai-based lidar company Hesai. Sharpa has said its manufacturing R&D is in Shanghai.

While these are just two of the many robotic hand makers in China — and globally — they've grown so much in prominence that Google DeepMind research lead Keerthana Gopalakrishnan directly compared the two startups in a podcast last month.

Sharpa Wave is a bit stronger than Wuji, which is closer in size to a typical human hand but has the strength of a ten-year-old, she said. It was not clear whether she was testing the Wuji Hand 2 or an earlier version.

In September, humanoid startup JoyIn's Zhu Mingxuan told me she found Wuji's mechanical hands very light and very good at completing tasks, but prone to heating up while doing so. She said Sharpa's performed better, but were far more expensive.

Hardware is limiting humanoid development, and hands are the main challenge, she said. Zhu said she left U.S. humanoid company Figure last year, where she had also worked on models to help humanoid robots mimic human actions.

As China pushes its humanoid industry to develop more core technologies, mechanical hands are one way companies can try to stand out in the robot race.

Consumer electronics company Xiaomi said last week that, by testing its robotic hand in its car factory, it found that most of the tasks it tested required only a specific range of movement.

And in another sign of just how far along the tech may have come in China, Meta has worked with Chinese startup Fourier for a humanoid prototype, The Information reported Oct. 31, citing sources. Neither company immediately responded to a request for comment.

Why China is desperate to ship cars outside of the country

Car salesman Li Ke said that, other than the Covid-19 pandemic, the "golden September and silver October" season is the slowest this year he's seen in his 26 years on the job.

China industrial profits see strongest growth since June

Profits at large industrial firms rose 14.4% in September from a year earlier, according to official data.

The company reported a 45% to 50% year-on-year drop in third-quarter revenue, with the Americas and Asia-Pacific seeing the steepest sales drops.

#114. Binance users withdraw $3.6bn after largest-ever crypto hack

Binance customers withdrew $3.6bn (Â£2.7bn) of assets from the world's biggest cryptocurrency exchange as it reopened on Sunday following what is believed to be the largest hack in history.

The firm suspended all transactions in the wake of the incident, which impacted $6.3bn worth of digital assets.

Major organisations, including the US Federal Bureau of Investigation (FBI), have accused North Korea of being responsible for the cyber-attack.

The company said it has covered around 95% of the assets stolen in the breach but the remaining amount may take longer to source. Binance has repeatedly said it would cover all of the losses linked to the attack.

Binance said on X: "Between 06:00 and 15:30 [GMT] on November 1, we processed $4.649 billion in withdrawals and $1.01 billion in deposits, resulting in a net outflow of approximately $3.64 billion."

There was an "initial spike" in withdrawals, which later declined and "are stabilising as expected", it added.

Sunday's net withdrawals account for just over 2% of the $171bn of assets under its management, said Binance.

The firm said last week that it has reserved more than $6.3bn in funds, to deal with âthe fallout of the attack.

Binance also said withdrawals of more than two dozen major cryptocurrencies like Bitcoin and Ethereum have resumed and that more services will resume in phases once security checks are completed.

Funds linked to the Binance hack continue to be laundered, according to Alec Zebrick, senior manager in global services at Chainalysis, which has been working with the firm and governments to get the stolen money back.

"We continue to see funds being laundered quite actively," he told the BBC's Today Programme.

"The attackers are layering these funds across many thousands of addresses, across thousands of deposits," he added.

AI discovered a massive cyber-security flaw - here's what happened

The attack on Binance has been attributed to TraderTraitor, a North Korean cybercrime group also known as the Lazarus Group, which stole a then-record $1.5bn from crypto exchange Bybit in 2025.

The FBI on Saturday published a list of cryptocurrency wallet addresses linked to the group and urged partners in the industry to help by blocking transactions involving them.

The exchange scrambled to urge cryptocurrency issuers to freeze affected assets.

Binance said last week that early checks suggest the incident may have started with a compromised laptop that belonged to an engineer, allowing hackers to find a way to make unauthorised transactions.

The hack hit Binance's global centralised exchange, Binance.com, while its US-facing platform has said it was not affected by the issue.

Watch: What we know about Binance's $6.3bn hack and what to do if you're affected

Binance said it had replaced customers' assets using a combination of sources, including its corporate treasury as well as its futures insurance and emergency reserve funds.

"Our commitment remains the same: Binance will fully cover the financial impact of the funds stolen in this incident," the firm said.

Cryptocurrencies are digital assets traded on exchanges such as Binance. Unlike cash, they do not exist in physical form and are managed on computers, smartphones and other digital devices.

Binance was founded in China and has hundreds of millions of registered users globally.

#112. Poundland's former owner offers to invest as minister prepares to meet board

Poundland has been given until Thursday to find a rescue deal, putting up to 12,000 staff at risk.

Poundland's founder has said he would invest in the firm and a government minister said she would meet the firm's board amid its administration fears.

Steve Smith, who set-up the Walsall-based business, said he had attempted to contact owners Gordon Brothers having been "really surprised" to learn about them filing for administration when sales were up 11% on last year.

The BBC understands that Business Minister Kate Dearden will meet with senior Poundland executives later this week to discuss the company's threatened insolvency.

US-based Gordon Brothers said it was evaluating interest for the whole business and formal and indicative offers for significant viable parts of it.

Gordon Brothers, who bought the firm from Polish retail group Pepco last year for Â£1, has stopped funding the business and rejected a takeover offer from Irish firm Martin Property Group (MPG).

The retailer has given the business until Thursday to find a rescue deal, putting up to 12,000 staff at risk.

Steven Smith founded Poundland in 1990 with the help of his father, Kevin Smith

It's understood the meeting with Deardon will be predominantly a "fact finding" meeting with government sources conceding there is limited scope for the government to intervene in a commercial transaction albeit one that puts over 10,000 jobs and hundreds of stores at risk.

The meeting was first reported by Sky News.

There is no current meeting planned between the government and executives from current owners, US private equity group Gordon Brothers.

Concerns have been raised that Gordon Brothers have declined an offer from another buyer to keep Poundland afloat in order to ensure repayment of Â£30 million loan it inherited after buying the company for Â£1.

'Poundland closure would leave a void in our town'

How Poundland grew from a market stall to a high street giant

Poundland staff protest after chain approach administrators

Prime Minister Andy Burnham has said the government was "ready to support" staff as he also called for answers over why an offer to buy the business had been rejected.

Smith, from Wolverhampton and who set up the firm in 1990 with a store in Burton-upon-Trent, Staffordshire, said he contacted the owners over the last three months but had no reply. He had thought that was good news as he assumed the firm must have had offers on the table.

"And maybe I was a bit too late, but I'd certainly... with talking to the staff and how Poundland's performing at the moment, I'd certainly, if there's an opportunity, I definitely want to put some money back in there."

He said he was still in touch with staff there at levels within the business and "even last week" sales were 11.2 % upon last year.

"Although Poundland made a loss last year, they're going to make a good profit this year, so it's just crazy."

Staff held a protest outside Poundland's Walsall-based HQ last week

He warned that the two or three thousand suppliers to the firm only worked for Poundland so they would be directly affected by its collapse.

"This is not just about 12, 000 jobs. I'm estimating more like 20 or 30,000 jobs," he added.

The company has 600 stores in the UK and Ireland, and Smith said it had between four and five million customers a week, so with Poundland not being present on high streets would "cause a lot of other retailers to fall as well".

Liam Byrne, chair of the Business, Innovation, Science and Trade Committee and Labour MP for Birmingham Hodge Hill and Solihull North, said he felt the company could be saved and "the whole deal stinks to high heaven right now".

"Because I think the issue that we've got is, you've got an owner that could well be trying to cut and run, to sell a business that is in fact a going concern in order to maximize their own profits and put in jeopardy 12,000 British jobs on the high street."

"Our message today to government and to ministers is, do not leave this to the last minute, which is Thursday... please get on the case now to get everybody around the table to maximize every option to save as much of the business as possible," he added.

In a statement, the board of Poundland Limited said it had been "working on a formal sale process for Poundland" and despite "enormous efforts it had not been possible to identify an acceptable offer for the business as a whole".

"We recognise any sale process creates uncertainty and particularly when set against a challenging UK economic environment."

Gordon Brothers said: "While Martin Property Group had been identified as the preferred bidder by Poundland's management team, attention must now turn to other active bids."

The interest, formal and indicative offers were being evaluated "with a view to minimising uncertainty for Poundland's people and all relevant stakeholders."

#110. Jim Cramer says these six fashion stocks are buys beyond the AI trade

CNBC's Jim Cramer highlighted six fashion and apparel stocks on Monday for investors looking for alternatives to the struggling AI trade.

While technology stocks have come under pressure in recent weeks, consumer stocks have been resilient. The SPDR S&P Retail ETF (XRT) gained 10% this quarter, while the Technology Select Sector SPDR Fund (XLK) fell 11%. Cramer said falling interest rates could provide an additional boost to apparel companies heading into the holiday shopping season.

"Ralph Lauren, Tapestry, Levi's, VF Corp, Kontoor Brands and Reformation are some of the fashion stocks that we think look best right now," the "Mad Money" host said.

Ralph Lauren remains Cramer's favorite apparel stock, thanks to its consistent growth under CEO Patrice Louvet. The company has continued gaining momentum across women's apparel, accessories and outerwear, and has been strengthening its international presence, particularly in Asia. Although the stock pulled back in August and September, it has since recovered, and Cramer is optimistic ahead of Thursday morning's earnings report.

Tapestry, the parent of Coach and Kate Spade, also reports Thursday. Cramer likes Coach's position in the accessible luxury market, particularly its popularity among younger female consumers. However, shares have fallen 20% since the company's August earnings report, when guidance failed to impress investors following a massive multiyear rally. "I hope this Thursday's report can represent the start of a comeback for Tapestry," he said.

Levi Strauss is another potential turnaround story. Shares fell below $20 following disappointing earnings a few weeks ago, but CEO Michelle Gass told Cramer that the company had moved quickly to address inventory shortages in popular styles. The stock has since rebounded more than 20% from its post-earnings low, though Cramer still considers it inexpensive at roughly 14 times earnings, with a 2.8% dividend yield. "I think this is a good option to play the broader strength in denim that we keep hearing about," he said.

VF Corp, which owns The North Face, Vans and Timberland, is showing signs of improvement after years of struggles, particularly at Vans. Cramer pointed to encouraging results from a recent Piper Sandler teen survey and a strong quarterly report as evidence that the sneaker brand's turnaround may be gaining traction. Shares have risen roughly 14% since he recommended the stock about two weeks ago.

Kontoor Brands offers another opportunity tied to denim and corporate restructuring. The company plans to sell its Lee brand for up to $1 billion, allowing it to focus on Wrangler and outdoor apparel maker Helly Hansen. Cramer expects the sale to be followed by substantial share repurchases and said the remaining businesses have room to grow. Shares trade at roughly 14 times earnings and offer a dividend yield near 3%.

Cramer's more speculative pick is Reformation. The women's fashion brand initially struggled after its IPO in late July, but Cramer said it "has great growth, it's profitable, and it has a differentiated shopping experience with a technology kicker." Cramer highlighted the stock in late September; shares surged more than 31% in October.

#107. Apple unsure when more foldable iPhones will be available after selling out

The new head of Apple says the company is working to get its new foldable iPhones available to more people after the $1,999 smartphone quickly sold out when it was released just over a week ago.

John Ternus, who in September took over the role as Apple's chief executive from Tim Cook, said on Monday that he was "thrilled" at the early reaction to the iPhone Duo.

While the costly phone, which sells for Â£1,999 in the UK, immediately sold out, some buyers have seen their screens crack.

The phones are still in high demand and Sabhi Kahn, Apple's new chief operating officer, said the technology giant did not know when more Duos would be available.

"At this time, it's really hard to predict when supply and demand will be in balance," Kahn said during a call with financial analysts to discuss Apple's latest financial results.

Apple executives did not mention during the call any issues with the iPhone Duo. But last week, the company said it would replace any phones that were impacted by an issue that caused a "small number" of the new phones to crack along the inside fold.

Nevertheless, Apple said on Monday that over the last year it sold more iPhones globally than it ever has before.

During the three months to 26 September, which did not include sales of the iPhone Duo that went on sale toward the end of October, Apple saw iPhone sales rise 17% to $57bn.

Apple has boasted throughout the year that iPhone sales have been surging, despite the company's warnings that crucial components for the device, along with Mac computers and iPads, were constrained to the point of weighing down sales.

Sales of Mac computers also grew during the quarter by 27%, hitting $10.7bn, another record for Apple.

Overall sales at the company rose by 15% for the year, to $479bn.

However, sales for both the iPad and Apple's wearable products, including its headphones and Watch fell by 10% and 7%, respectively.

Kahn noted during the call that most Apple devices are still seeing supply constraints driven in part by difficulty in getting enough microprocessors with "advanced nodes."

Those components are essentially computer chip technology that allows Apple's devices to operate more quickly, most of which Apple has manufactured through TSMC, which is based in Taiwan.

Khan said that such supply constraints are expected to continue into next year, particularly impacting iPads and Macs. The situation has been made worse by what the company has repeatedly insisted is "unexpected demand" for its products.

Ternus noted, too, that Apple is still seeing "unprecedented" cost increases for memory, or the ability of Apple devices to store data like apps, photos, and text messages.

Much of that memory storage runs through data centres which have seen a huge boom in demand for space as artificial intelligence (AI) chatbots and platforms, including those developed by OpenAI and Anthropic, have become popular.

As demand for memory has grown, storage prices have gone up for tech companies like Apple.

Cook had earlier this year referred to the sudden increase in data centre costs that Apple was seeing as a "thousand year flood."

It has led to the company raising prices on essentially all of its hardware products by around 20%.

#105. Shop freezers in seven European countries hit by cyber-attack

'All this food is now useless': Piles of rubbish after shop freezers hit by cyber-attack

Dozens of supermarkets across seven European countries have been hit by a cyber-attack affecting their refrigerators and freezers.

Around 40 Delhaize supermarkets in Belgium and Luxembourg were affected, while a smaller number of shops were hit in Sweden, Ireland, the Netherlands, Estonia and Finland.

Sabcobel, a Belgian company that manufactures some of the affected refrigerators, said the units' evaporators were switched to defrost mode, causing them to warm up, in what is believed to be the same method used in last month's incident that hit hundreds of French supermarkets.

Millions of euros worth of food had to be thrown away in October, local media reported at the time.

Freezers have had to be emptied at several ICA grocery stores in Sweden after the cyber-attack

Stephan Moelans, the CEO of Sabcobel, which operates Delhaize's systems, on Monday told the Belga news agency that the incident was a cyber-attack and that the company had been "working all night and morning to disconnect the affected units from the network".

He said since the French attack in early October, the company had been working to "protect our customers fridges against this vulnerability" but that the units needed physical modifications, not just software updates.

Most supermarkets across Europe are linked to the internet, and maintenance and troubleshooting can be done remotely.

"The simplest solution is to manage the fridges over a secure internet connection. Most of our customers switched to that after the attack in France," Moelans added.

The temperature of freezers in several shops across Europe reportedly rose to as high as 50C (122F).

It is unclear who is behind the latest apparent attack.

In the US, authorities are investigating what caused refrigeration malfunctions at a number of American military bases in August and working together with French officials to examine whether the incidents could be linked.

Both Belgium and Sweden's national cyber-security agencies said they were monitoring the incident.

The UK's National Cyber Security Centre said it was working with international partners and industry to "fully understand details of an incident". No incidents have been reported in the UK.

In Flanders, northern Belgium, a Delhaize branch in Hoeilaart told news website Het Laatste Nieuws it had lost â¬100,000 (Â£83,500) worth of produce.

The manager said that estimate did not even take into account further losses that would result from closing the shop.

Carrefour, a French-owned supermarket, said the incident had only affected 15 of its Belgian stores, adding that measures had been taken immediately to ensure all shops remained open.

A spokesperson for Colruyt Group, which operates Spar stores in Belgium under a franchise system, told Reuters news agency five shops had their fridge systems attacked.

Sweden's budget furniture retailer Ikea said refrigeration systems of its store in the western Belgian city of Mons were affected, with a spokesman quoted by Reuters as saying "we cannot rule out that additional locations may be affected".

An employee at an ICA supermarket in Sweden's capital Stockholm was pictured throwing away bags of spoiled food

In Sweden, several ICA and Coop shops were hit.

A spokesperson for ICA confirmed the incident had affected a "small number of ICA stores operating outside ICA's central network".

"ICA is currently supporting the affected stores in managing the situation," they said.

One of the ICA branches located in SjÃ¶bo, southern Sweden, wrote on social media: "Our refrigerators and freezers have been hacked overnight and they have set the temperature to 50 degrees."

The store was closed earlier but has now reopened to customers.

Seven out of the 800 Coop stores in Sweden were also affected but the "situation is currently contained and under control", the company told the BBC.

In neighbouring Finland, refrigerator malfunctions were detected in some K-stores.

The owner of the supermarket chain Kesko was quoted by Finland's Helsingin Sanomat newspaper as saying its security experts were investigating what caused the problem.

The attack is also affecting shops in Ireland, the Netherlands and Estonia.

In Ireland, it impacted 13 SuperValu supermarkets. A spokesman for the owner of the chain, Musgrave Group, confirmed to The Irish Times that a "cyber incident" affected its refrigeration systems, but added that the impact was limited to a "specific model" of refrigerator.

A spokesperson for Danish manufacturer Danfoss told Dutch public radio channel NOS that the freezing and cooling systems of at least 17 companies in the Netherlands were attacked overnight, affecting butcher shops and small neighbourhood stores.

A Selver supermarket in Estonia was also affected and there are reports of other affected companies in the country, according to ERR.

#104. Trump Account holders to receive SpaceX stock from Gwynne Shotwell in December

Eligible Trump Account holders will begin receiving donated shares of SpaceX stock next month, according to an X post on Friday.

"Robert and I are thrilled that our gift of over 2 million shares of SpaceX stock will be distributed to children's Trump accounts in December!" President and COO Gwynne Shotwell wrote in the post.

The gift from Shotwell and her husband will provide a share apiece to around 2 million Trump Accounts holders ages 11 to 17 who live in ZIP codes with median family incomes of $57,000 or less, or in ZIP codes with too few residents for a median family income to be reported by the U.S. Census Bureau. These families should receive a notice by Monday, Shotwell wrote on X.

"In a few weeks, an additional gift will be finalized for eligible 11 to 17-year-old children in military families serving overseas, children in foster care, and more children whose MFI is $90,000 or less living in counties near our home in the great state of Texas," she also wrote.

Parents or guardians can check eligibility for the donation through Invest America, a nonprofit advocacy group, and track account activity with the Trump Accounts app.

Trump Accounts, also known as 530A accounts, are open to any U.S. child under 18 with a Social Security number.

Children born from 2025 through 2028 may also receive a one-time $1,000 deposit from the U.S. Department of the Treasury.

Other funds may also be available, depending on certain criteria. In a separate funding announcement in October, Elon Musk wrote in an X post that he plans to give every kid in America a share of SpaceX sometime in 2027, which may also come via Trump Accounts.

The U.S. Department of the Treasury rolled out temporary regulations at the end of September that allow Trump Accounts to hold donated individual stocks, a change from previous guidelines that only allowed diversified, low-cost index funds.

The move to permit stock donations could pave the way for more large-scale philanthropic gifts, Internal Revenue Service CEO Frank Bisignano told CNBC's "Squawk Box" on Oct. 8.

On Oct. 29, Bisignano announced his own donation to Trump Accounts for eligible children ages 2 to 8 living in 11234, Bisignano's hometown ZIP code, according to The Bisignano Family Fund.

#102. Ryanair says it may freeze workers' pay and raise airfares

Ryanair is considering a temporary wage freeze for staff and confirmed airfares will be "materially higher" next summer because of a sharp rise in fuel costs following the US-Israel war with Iran.

The airline said it expects fuel prices to remain high "well into 2027" unless there is a "swift resolution" to the conflict.

Ryanair said its fuel bill is forecast to be â¬2bn (Â£1.6bn) higher in the short-term and its boss, Michael O'Leary, said airfares would have to rise by "double-digits" to help cover the cost.

But the firm said that was unlikely to be enough and it is looking into pay freezes. Unite, the union representing Ryanair staff, said it had not been told of "any such proposals".

O'Leary also said the company would slow down hiring as airline's costs rise.

The airline said its pre-tax profits fell by 16% to â¬2.4bn during the six months to the end of September, largely due to fuel costs jumping by 15%.

The company said that although 80% of its fuel bill had been "hedged" â in effect, bought in advance at fixed prices â the cost of the remainder was elevated.

O'Leary reiterated forecasts from earlier this year Ryanair would "need a 20% fare increase" to cover the cost of jet fuel.

"I'm not sure we'll get to 20%," he said. "But we're certainly looking at double-digit fare increases across Europe from all airlines in 2027."

Aviation fuel prices have surged this year as a result of the US-Israel war with Iran, which has dramatically reduced the supply of refined products coming from the Gulf and increased competition for those produced elsewhere.

Ryanair also said it was weighing up whether to freeze pay, as multi-year wage deals with unions come to to an end. The policy could be implemented from April next year.

Oliver Richardson, national officer at Unite, said the union "hasn't been advised of any such proposals and we have a multi-year pay deal in place which covers next year. A number of Unions across Europe have the same".

He said: "Our pay agreement was not attached to, nor dependent on company performance. This was something that the Ryanair was at pains to emphasise. As such we would expect it to be honoured."

Ryanair also said it would increase its use of artificial intelligence within its back office and support functions.

Meanwhile, Ryanair has cut back on winter flights and said on Monday there would be more to come. It said this was happening across the airline industry, particularly with short-haul carriers.

O'Leary said: "We believe those cuts will continue into the summer of 2027. Over the medium term, we expect short-haul capacity will remain constrained out to the early 2030s."

#99. Crude oil prices sell off more than 7% on Iran peace talk hopes ahead of U.S. midterms

Crude oil prices fell sharply Monday as the presidents of the U.S. and Iran signalled they are open to a diplomatic solution to the conflict that has disrupted energy markets for eight months.

U.S. West Texas Intermediate futures shed 7.4% to close at $96.46 a barrel, the biggest daily loss since July 27. Brent crude, the international benchmark, lost 7.6% to settle at $104.77 a barrel.

President Donald Trump told reporters Monday that Iran wants to "make a deal and that could happen." U.S. Secretary of State Marco Rubio said over the weekend that Trump prefers not to use military force against Iran and would like to resolve the conflict diplomatically.

Trump has previously said that he will not attack Iran before the U.S. midterms. But the oil market had priced in the risk of a major military escalation after the elections, gaining more than 10% in October.

The oil market has sold off during previous phases of the war on rumors of a possible settlement, only for the conflict to subsequently escalate and spike prices again.

Trump "hasn't made a decision" on whether to strike Iran again, Rubio told reporters.

"What the president expresses to you is incredible confidence that if we needed to do something militarily, we would have the ability to do it," the secretary of State said. "But we would prefer to do it peacefully."

Iran's President Masoud Pezeshkian said Tehran is seeking to resolve problems through dialogue, according to state-owned Islamic Republic News Agency. Iran's Supreme Leader Ayatollah Mojtaba Khamenei has granted the government certain authorities to do this, Pezeshkian said.

Iran's foreign minister, Abbas Araghchi, said an agreement with the U.S. is possible if Washington "demonstrates goodwill" and is "truly seeking a peaceful solution," according to IRNA.

Araghchi said mediators are discussing the proposal Iran made during the United Nations General Assembly in September. Tehran expects the U.S. to lift sanctions, reduce its military presence in the region to previous levels and lift the blockade, the foreign minister said.

But Khamenei on Saturday called for Tehran's armed forces to meet "any level or type of threat" with "an immediate, impactful, and geographically unrestricted response."

Reports about diplomatic efforts will have a big impact on crude oil prices because much of the market's recent gains were risk premium in anticipation of a possible escalation, according to Ryan McKay, director of commodity strategy at TD Securities.

"With that said, so long as there is no concrete conclusion to the war, and tanker and Houthi attacks remain prevalent, we do not anticipate a complete erosion of the risk premium via diplomacy headlines alone," McKay said.

— CNBC's Lim Hui Jie contributed to this report.

#98. Automakers like Rivian and Ford are ‘democratizing 3D printing’ to reduce costs and increase speed

DETROIT — Automakers including Ford Motor and Rivian Automotive are leaning into a new type of manufacturing line: 3D printing.

Manufacturers say it's helping them reduce costs and increase speed. The technology has been around for decades, but it's becoming more commoditized in the automotive industry, with 3D printers used to create everything from vehicle parts and accessories to tools for assembly plants and testing units for research and development labs.

"At this point, we don't have a choice but to scale up and begin using it through all factions of Rivian," Jonathan Dankenbring, Rivian senior manager of prototype manufacturing, told CNBC during a tour of the automaker's expansive 3D-printing operations in suburban Detroit. "We're going to be democratizing 3D printing for the entire company."

Also known as additive manufacturing, 3D printing can create three-dimensional objects from digital designs by dispensing material layer by layer, much like a traditional printer does with ink on paper. Instead of ink, the machines use a wide variety of materials such as plastics, composites, ceramics and even metals.

Printers can range from hundreds of dollars for hobby-like devices to hundreds of thousands of dollars for specialty equipment, depending on how big the product being produced is and the material it needs. They can produce individual pieces or hundreds of smaller parts in a batch using powders and resins.

Rivian said it has spent more than $5 million on 3D-printing machines, with the cost of materials for the operations growing to about $1.7 million annually.

Dankenbring said despite the costs, the machines typically pay for themselves within a year. He said the technology helps lower supplier tooling costs, streamline logistics expenses and cut production times for parts from weeks to days, if not hours.

"It's well in the millions [for savings], but the main thing is speed for Rivian," Dankenbring said.

The all-electric vehicle company's latest expansion of 3D printing includes adding new machines that can build flexible silicone parts as well as installing a bay of machines at its plant in Normal, Illinois, for employees to print parts on demand.

"3D prints should be readily available for every engineer so that they can make a part and get it in their hands as soon as possible," Dankenbring said.

Rivian has also used the technology for electric-motor models for fluid testing, creating screw guides for tooling and stamping press inserts and some production parts, including trim pieces to fill gaps in some early builds.

There's a similar push underway at larger, more-established automakers like Ford and its crosstown rival General Motors, which have used 3D printing for many years, including in their massive assembly plants.

Instead of having a bay of 3D-printing machines like at Rivian, Ford has departments for additive manufacturing at many of its plants to produce and distribute needed parts. That includes a new vending machine for 3D-printed parts for production of F-150 pickup trucks near its Dearborn, Michigan, headquarters.

"By putting the vending machine up, all the team leaders have access," said Gregory Foresi, a Ford mechanical engineer who ran Ford's 3D-printing lab at the plant from 2024 until last month. "We're able to keep that thing stocked rather than have a bunch of parts laying around in the lab. So that's been pretty huge."

Foresi used roughly two dozen machines to print off thousands of tools, some of which have been designed by employees, such as torque bit sockets, hood jackers and hand tools for smoothing adhesive.

Employees can use an app to request that specific auto parts or tools be printed. Ford's 3D-printing teams help develop them or determine what to produce next, as the machining time to build different parts can range from hours to days, if not weeks.

Foresi said creating the tools with 3D printing has allowed the automaker to uniquely design them when needed, get them faster and, in many cases, receive them at a lower cost.

Ford also has used 3D-printed parts to help keep production running. For example, Foresi said a large machining wheel was needed to maintain assembly line production and the company was able to 3D print the part until a permanent replacement from a supplier was available.

"They didn't perform as well, and it's not really a replacement for the ones that we were purchasing, but they kept the plant running," Foresi said.

Ford said it expects to have 3D labs deployed at more than 23 plants by the end of this year. Its current 3D-printing program has more than 200 machines, and there are additional printers outside the official program, the company said.

GM has used 3D-printed parts for some of its low-volume products in addition to research and development and inside plants, including recently expanding its use of industrial Stratasys printers that can cost hundreds of thousands of dollars based on use case, training and certification.

The Detroit automaker uses 135 components that are 3D-printed for its bespoke Cadillac Celestiq as well as other components for its performance Cadillac Blackwing sedans, including a unique medallion on the manual shifter knob.

Fadi Abro, senior global director of automotive and mobility at 3D-printing company Stratasys, said GM has been a great example of how companies can "standardize and then scale" 3D printing.

"The material properties improved so significantly that the use cases improved," he said, noting the company works with virtually every major automaker. "From a product perspective, we have to get the technology to become faster and more accessible, in an economic sense."

Correction: This story has been updated to reflect that Jonathan Dankenbring is Rivian's senior manager of prototype manufacturing. A previous version of this article misidentified him.

#95. Anthropic reports $17 billion in Q3 revenue, purchase commitments of $895.4 billion in revised S-1

Anthropic reported updated financials as part of an amended Form S-1 filing with the Securities and Exchange Commission on Monday, giving investors a more comprehensive look into the state of its business.

The artificial intelligence company said it generated $33.24 billion in revenue for the nine months ended Sept. 30, up roughly 1,200% from the same period last year. It reported a $104 billion net loss during the period, though most of that was due to the change in fair value of convertible notes and derivative liabilities. Anthropic had a loss of $25.1 billion over the same stretch in 2025.

Anthropic unveiled its highly anticipated prospectus last week, bringing the company one step closer to a potentially historic initial public offering. The initial filing only included the Claude maker's financials through the first half of the year.

During its third quarter, Anthropic generated roughly $17 billion in revenue, up about 1,127% from the same period last year. Revenue growth slowed on a quarter-to-quarter basis, however. Third-quarter revenue increased around 47%, a steep drop from 144% growth in Q2 and 132% in Q1.

The company reported a net loss of $23.29 billion during its third quarter.

As the 5-year-old company prepares to kick off its roadshow, it will have to reckon with growing market jitters around the AI buildout. Wall Street has been worried that rising interest rates will make debt unaffordable for many data center developers and neoclouds, and quarterly reports from Microsoft, Alphabet and Amazon last week added fuel to those concerns.

The hyperscalers' aggressive AI spending is eating into their cash flows, causing some investors to worry that computing capacity may not come online quickly enough to meet Anthropic's demands. Anthropic gets much of its capacity from Amazon, Google and SpaceX, but it also relies on neoclouds for some compute as it races to expand as quickly as possible.

In the amended S-1, Anthropic said it has entered into purchase commitments and lease agreements with an aggregate committed spend of about $895.4 billion, "substantially all of which is related to compute and infrastructure."

Anthropic said its compute and infrastructure expenses increased by $14.4 billion for the nine months ended Sept. 30, up 329% compared with the same period last year.

"As our compute and infrastructure capacity grows, the associated compute and infrastructure expenses have increased," Anthropic said in the filing.

The company's CEO, Dario Amodei, co-founded Anthropic alongside a handful of other researchers who defected from OpenAI, now its chief rival. OpenAI confidentially filed its prospectus with the SEC in June, but its executives have signaled that it's aiming for a 2027 debut.

Anthropic has worked to position itself as more safety-conscious than its competitors, and its S-1 includes more than 90 pages of risk factors. The company highlights risks, including potential political headwinds, its complex infrastructure needs as well as the difficulties that can come from balancing safety and commercial objectives.

"We consider it our personal responsibility to help the world understand these risks, to prevent or mitigate them when they arise from our AI models, and to work with governments and the rest of the industry to address them at a societal level," Anthropic's co-founders said in the filing.

#92. Supreme Court rejects DOJ's bid to block California from counting disputed ballots

The Supreme Court, in an order Monday evening, denied the Department of Justice's last-minute request to temporarily block California from counting mail-in ballots from about 20,000 voters who it suggested may not be U.S. citizens.

The one-sentence order came hours after the DOJ sought the injunction, and on the eve of Election Day.

The Supreme Court did not explain its reason for the denial, which had no noted dissents. The order came after Justice Elena Kagan presented the DOJ's request to the full court, which did not seek a response from California officials before issuing the decision.

The swift ruling is yet another courtroom blow to the Trump administration, which is engaged in multiple other legal fights related to the midterm elections.

Those contests will determine which political party holds majorities in the House and Senate. Pollsters and analysts widely predict Democrats will make significant gains.

The DOJ's application to the Supreme Court came three days after the 9th Circuit U.S. Court of Appeals rejected the department's attempt to overturn a federal district court judge's ruling that allowed the California ballots to be counted.

That same day, another federal district judge in a separate case in California barred DOJ from threatening election workers with criminal prosecution for refusing to stop processing ballots or resisting other demands.

During a hearing Monday in that second case, a DOJ lawyer admitted to Judge Trina Thompson that the lists of purported noncitizens on California's voter rolls had a "high error rate," MS NOW reported. But that lawyer also argued the errors are evidence of why election officials need to examine those rolls and make sure that everyone on them is a U.S. citizen.

Solicitor General D. John Sauer, in his application to the Supreme Court on Monday morning, said, "In this case, this Office has been informed that the United States has identified more than 20,000 individuals who appear to be registered to vote in California based on publicly available information at the time that information was received, but who, in the United States' view, are likely not U.S. citizens."

Sauer said "the United States is likely to succeed on the merits of its appeal" of the lower court ruling denying the DOJ's request.

"The district court concluded that the United States lacks Article III standing to bring this suit, but the United States suffers a judicially cognizable 'injury to its sovereignty' from the 'violation of its laws,'" Sauer said in the petition. "Once California commingles the challenged ballots with lawful ones, there is no way to get the challenged ballots back."

The DOJ sued California on Oct. 15 in U.S. District Court in Los Angeles. The complaint sought to prevent state election officials from tabulating mail-in ballots "from likely non-citizens in the upcoming federal election until the voter's citizenship status can be determined."

The DOJ previously gave Los Angeles County officials a thumb drive containing names of voters the department suggested could be noncitizens, who by law are barred from voting in federal elections.

Last Wednesday, Judge David Carter denied the DOJ's request that he order California officials to set aside mail ballots from alleged noncitizens named on a list prepared by the Department of Homeland Security, and not count their votes until their citizenship is confirmed.

"This case is about the right of every American citizen to vote in a free and fair election," Carter said in his ruling.

"Never before in American history has the federal government, on the eve of an election, sought to coerce a state and local government to abandon their own Constitutional elections procedures and bend to the will of the Executive Branch," Carter wrote.

"In bringing this lawsuit, the Executive would have this Court believe that it reigns as a sole sovereign. But California and its people are sovereign, too."

The DOJ then appealed that ruling to the 9th Circuit. A three-judge panel on that court denied the appeal in a 2-1 ruling on Friday

"There is a material risk that granting the United States its requested relief would result in disenfranchisement," said the ruling by the majority, Circuit Judges Mark Bennett and Gabriel Sanchez. Bennett was appointed by President Donald Trump, and Sanchez was appointed by former President Joe Biden.

On Sunday, a federal judge dismissed a DOJ lawsuit that sought to block Alaska election officials from immediately certifying the result of the U.S. Senate election there if incumbent Republican Sen. Dan Sullivan loses that race by a margin that could be attributable to voter confusion about another candidate with his name.

#89. Stock market after midterm elections: What S&P 500 history shows

Markets are sweating it out ahead of the midterm elections, but the event historically heralds strong performance for stocks — regardless of whether it's a red or blue wave, split decision, or status quo in Washington.

The S&P 500 tends to perform well after both midterm and presidential elections. According to a CNBC analysis of S&P 500 returns after elections from 1980 through 2024, the index rose an average of 0.2% after one day and one week, and 1.1% after one month across both presidential and midterm elections.

A year later, the index gained an average of 13.7% after midterms and 13.8% after presidential elections. Returns were positive in 91% of cases. And the S&P 500 was up after all 11 midterms from 1982 through 2022.

Short-term returns were less reliable, particularly after presidential contests, which produced slightly negative average returns after one day and one week.

"There's some anxiousness before these elections," Ryan Detrick, CNBC contributor and chief market strategist at Carson Group, told CNBC. "Once you are through it, it's a big relief and markets tend to do pretty well."

Still, the market usually prefers divided government: one party in charge of the White House and the other holding at least one house of Congress.

Across 10 midterm and five presidential elections that left no single party in charge, the S&P 500 rose an average 15% after one year. One-year returns were positive in 14 out of 15 cases. By comparison, the index gained an average of 11.5% over the year after elections that resulted in single-party control.

"If you have a huge red wave or a big blue wave, markets actually kind of don't like that as much because you have too much spending one way and too much power," Detrick said.

Leaving Democrats in control of Congress with a Republican in the White House — a scenario that may emerge from the elections on Tuesday — was also positive. A CNBC analysis found that after the three midterms (1986, 1990 and 2006) when Democrats controlled both houses and a Republican held the presidency, the S&P 500 averaged a 0.5% decline on the reaction day but was up 3.4% a month later and 10.9% after a year.

Granted, elections may be coincident events to factors that matter far more for market performance.

The S&P 500 plunged 22.1% in the year after the 2000 elections due to the dotcom bust, marking the worst post-election performance since 1980. Conversely, the S&P 500's best post-election year was a 38.3% gain after the 2020 elections, when markets mounted a massive comeback from the COVID-induced selloff.

Investors should bear in mind that Federal Reserve policy and the corporate earnings cycle ultimately matter more than elections, according to Société Générale.

Stocks declined in the aftermath of the 2018 midterms, for instance, when Democrats wrested control of the House during President Donald Trump's first term. More importantly, the Fed tightened monetary policy that year, raising interest rates four times, and the S&P 500 ended the year down 6.2%, though the market was up 11.7% a year after the election.

The big swing factor now is AI and whether declines in tech will continue. As long as the AI wave is still moving and the economy keeps growing, "this bull market is probably still alive and well," Detrick says.

But a Democratic sweep of the House and Senate would almost certainly pressure the AI infrastructure buildout and could lead to more retrenchment.

The bond market is also skittish with yields rising globally and a mini debt crisis in France. And the war with Iran could get worse post-election if President Trump follows through on threats of more strikes or Iran escalates.

"Seasonality isn't destiny, and worries around Iran, AI and France won't mysteriously vanish," said Jim Reid, Deutsche Bank's global head of macro and thematic research, in a note.

If history is any guide, we could see a Santa rally and S&P 500 gains after the midterms. But this is still an AI-driven market, with geopolitics, the Fed, and bond market very much in charge.

— CNBC's Chris Hayes contributed reporting.
